by the National Science Foundation (Grant No. DMS-1925919).

2.
Notations and crash intro into the s-embedding formalism

We concisely recall the general construction of s-embeddings introduced in
[
15
, Section 3]
, along with the regularity theory of the so-called s-holomorphic functions, both derived from a complexification of the standard Kadanoff-Ceva formalism. Our notation follows precisely that of
[
15
,
61
,
62
]
and is consistent with
[
17
, Section 3]
,
[
16
]
, and
[
23
,
22
]
. As we do not provide proofs, we refer the reader to
[
15
, Section 2]
for further details. Chelkak’s original idea was to construct a class of embeddings associated with a given weighted abstract graph, where the weights carry a geometric interpretation, enabling the application of discrete complex analysis techniques.

Figure 1
.
(Left) Notation for a given quad
z\in\diamondsuit(G)
with an arbitrary embedding in the plane. Vertices of the primal graph
G^{\bullet}
are shown as black dots, while vertices of the dual graph
G^{\circ}
, corresponding to the faces of
G
, are represented as white dots. The so-called corners, corresponding to the edges of the bipartite graph
\Lambda(G)=G^{\bullet}\cup G^{\circ}
, are depicted as triangles. This figure illustrates a portion of the
double cover
of the corner graph, branching around
z
. Corners that are neighbors
in this double cover
are connected by dashed lines. (Right) A portion of the associated
s
-embedding containing the quad
\mathcal{S}^{\diamondsuit}(z)
, tangent to a circle with radius
r_{z}
centered at
\mathcal{S}(z)
. The Ising weight of the edge between the vertices
v_{0}^{\bullet}
and
v_{1}^{\bullet}
can be recovered using the four angles
\phi_{v,z}
associated with the quad
\mathcal{S}^{\diamondsuit}(z)
, following the formula in (
2.10
).

2.1.
Notation and Kadanoff–Ceva formalism

Let us fix G as a planar graph, allowing multi-edges and vertices of degree 2 but not loops or vertices of degree 1, with the combinatorics of either the plane or the sphere. The graph G is considered up to homeomorphisms that preserve the cyclic order of edges around each vertex. In the spherical case, one designates a particular face of G as the outer face.

We denote by
G=G^{\bullet}
the original graph, where vertices are represented by
v^{\bullet}\in G^{\bullet}
, and by
G^{\circ}
its dual, where vertices
v^{\circ}\in G^{\circ}
correspond to the faces of G. The faces of the graph
\Lambda(G):=G^{\circ}\cup G^{\bullet}
, which has a natural incidence relation as a bipartite graph, are in straightforward bijection with the edges of G. Additionally, we denote by
\diamondsuit(G)
the graph dual to
\Lambda(G)
, where vertices, labeled as
z\in\diamondsuit(G)
, correspond to its faces. This graph is commonly referred to as the quad graph. Finally, we define
\Upsilon(G)
as the medial graph of
\Lambda(G)
, whose vertices—called the corners of G—are in direct bijection with the edges
(v^{\bullet}v^{\circ})
of
\Lambda(G)
.

To ensure the full consistency of the Kadanoff-Ceva formalism, it is generally necessary to work with various double covers of the graph
\Upsilon(G)
. For relevant illustrations of these double covers, see, for example,
[
67
, Fig. 27]
or
[
15
, Fig 3.A]
. In this paper, we denote by
\Upsilon^{\times}(G)
the double cover that branches over
all
the faces of
\Upsilon(G)
, meaning around each element of the type
v^{\bullet}\in G^{\bullet},v^{\circ}\in G^{\circ}
, and
z\in\diamondsuit(G)
. When G is finite, this definition remains meaningful since the quantity
\#(G^{\bullet})+\#(G^{\circ})+\#(\diamondsuit(G))
is always even.
Given a set
\varpi=\{v^{\bullet}_{1},\ldots,v^{\bullet}_{m},v^{\circ}_{1},\ldots,v^{\circ}_{n}\}\subset\Lambda(G)
, where both integers n and m are even, we define
\Upsilon^{\times}_{\varpi}(G)
as the double cover of
\Upsilon(G)
that branches over all its faces
except
those in
\varpi
. Similarly, we denote by
\Upsilon_{\varpi}(G)
the double cover of
\Upsilon(G)
that branches
only
over the faces in
\varpi
. A function defined on any of these double covers is called a
spinor
if its values at two different lifts of the same corner differ solely by a sign, i.e., by a multiplicative factor of
-1
.

In this paper, we work with the Ising model on the faces of the graph
G
, including the outer face in the disc case, which corresponds to starting with
wired
boundary conditions. This statistical mechanics model generates a random assignment of
\pm 1
variables to the vertices of
G^{\circ}
, governed by the partition function (
1.1
). The associated low-temperature expansion
[
17
, Section 1.2]
maps a spin configuration
\sigma:G^{\circ}\to\{\pm 1\}
to a subset
C
of edges in
G
that separate spins of opposite sign. This mapping is, in fact, a
2
-to-
1
correspondence, depending on the value assigned to the spin at the outer face.

One can fix an even number
n
of vertices
v^{\circ}_{1},\ldots,v^{\circ}_{n}\in G^{\circ}
and consider a subgraph
\gamma^{\circ}=\gamma_{[v^{\circ}_{1},\ldots,v^{\circ}_{n}]}\subset G^{\circ}
that has odd degree only at the vertices of
v^{\circ}_{1},\ldots,v^{\circ}_{n}
and even degree at all other vertices of
G^{\circ}
. Such a configuration can be interpreted as a collection of paths on
G^{\circ}
that pairwise connect the vertices in
v^{\circ}_{1},\ldots,v^{\circ}_{n}
. Denoting

x_{[v^{\circ}_{1},\ldots,v^{\circ}_{n}]}(e)\ :=\ (-1)^{e\cdot\gamma_{[v^{\circ}_{1},\ldots,v^{\circ}_{n}]}}\,x(e),\quad e\in E(G),

where
e\cdot\gamma=0
if the edge
e
doesn’t cross the path
\gamma
and
e\cdot\gamma=1
otherwise. It is possible to reconstruct the correlation formula

\textstyle\mathbb{E}\big[\sigma_{v^{\circ}_{1}}\ldots\sigma_{v^{\circ}_{n}}\big]\ =\ {x_{[v^{\circ}_{1},\ldots,v^{\circ}_{n}]}(\mathcal{E}(G))}\big/{x(\mathcal{E}(G))},

(2.1)

where
x(C):=\prod_{e\in C}x(e)
,
x(\mathcal{E}(G)):=\sum_{c\in\mathcal{E}(G)}x(C)
, and similarly for product of the kind
x_{[v^{\circ}_{1},\ldots,v^{\circ}_{n}]}
.

If
m
is again even and
v^{\bullet}_{1},\ldots,v^{\bullet}_{m}\in G^{\bullet}
, one can fix a subgraph
\gamma^{\bullet}=\gamma^{[v^{\bullet}_{1},\ldots,v^{\bullet}_{m}]}\subset G^{\bullet}
with even degree at all the vertices of
G^{\bullet}
, except those belonging to
v^{\bullet}_{1},\ldots,v^{\bullet}_{m}
. In the spirit of the Kadanoff-Ceva formalism
[
50
]
, one can change the signs of the interaction constants
J_{e}\mapsto-J_{e}
on edges
e\in\gamma^{\bullet}
, which is equivalent to replacing
x(e)
by
x(e)^{-1}
along the edges of
\gamma^{\bullet}
, thereby making the model anti-ferromagnetic near
\gamma^{\bullet}
. This leads to the random variable (which still depends on the choice of
\gamma^{\bullet}
):

\textstyle\mu_{v^{\bullet}_{1}}\ldots\mu_{v^{\bullet}_{m}}\ :=\ \exp\big[-2\beta\sum_{e\in\gamma^{[v^{\bullet}_{1},\ldots,v^{\bullet}_{m}]}}J_{e}\sigma_{v^{\circ}_{-}(e)}\sigma_{v^{\circ}_{+}(e)}\,\big]\,.

The domain walls representation gives that (e.g.
[
17
, Propositon 1.3]
)

\textstyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mu_{v^{\bullet}_{1}}\ldots\mu_{v^{\bullet}_{m}}\big]\ =\ x(\mathcal{E}^{[v^{\bullet}_{1},\ldots,v^{\bullet}_{m}]}(G))\big/{x(\mathcal{E}(G))},

(2.2)

where
\mathcal{E}^{[v^{\bullet}_{1},\ldots,v^{\bullet}_{m}]}
is the set of subgraphs with even degree at all vertices, except for those in
v^{\bullet}_{1},\ldots,v^{\bullet}_{m}
, which have odd degrees. Taking the expectation in (
2.2
), the result no longer depends on
\gamma^{\bullet}
. The key observation is that one can generalize (
2.1
) and (
2.2
) to the case where both spins and disorder are present simultaneously, which now reads as (e.g.
[
17
, Propositon 3.3]
):

\textstyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mu_{v^{\bullet}_{1}}\ldots\mu_{v^{\bullet}_{m}}\sigma_{v^{\circ}_{1}}\ldots\sigma_{v^{\circ}_{n}}\big]\ =\ x_{[v^{\circ}_{1},\ldots,v^{\circ}_{n}]}(\mathcal{E}^{[v^{\bullet}_{1},\ldots,v^{\bullet}_{m}]}(G))\big/{x(\mathcal{E}(G))},

(2.3)

where the variable
\mu_{v^{\bullet}_{1}}\ldots\mu_{v^{\bullet}_{m}}
retains the same definition as above. However, an additional difficulty arises for these mixed correlations. Specifically, the sign of the last expression now depends on the parity of the number of intersections between the paths
\gamma^{\circ}
and
\gamma^{\bullet}
. There is no canonical way to fix this sign in (
2.3
) while staying within the Cartesian product structure
(G^{\bullet})^{\times m}\times(G^{\circ})^{\times n}
. To circumvent this issue, one can fix
\mathcal{S}:\Lambda(G)\to\mathbb{C}
, an arbitrarily chosen embedding of
G
, and consider the natural double cover of
(G^{\bullet})^{\times m}\times(G^{\circ})^{\times n}
, which branches exactly as the spinor
\left[\prod_{p=1}^{m}\prod_{q=1}^{n}(\mathcal{S}(v^{\bullet}_{p})-\mathcal{S}(v^{\circ}_{q}))\right]^{1/2}
. Following the detailed discussion in
[
19
, Section 2.2]
, the expressions in (
2.3
) are spinors on
\left[\prod_{p=1}^{m}\prod_{q=1}^{n}(\mathcal{S}(v^{\bullet}_{p})-\mathcal{S}(v^{\circ}_{q}))\right]^{1/2}
. When working with mixed correlations of the form (
2.3
), the usual Kramers-Wannier duality (see again
[
17
, Propositon 3.3]
) implies that
G^{\bullet}
and
G^{\circ}
play equivalent roles.

Among all possible correlators of the form (
2.3
), one can focus on the special case where one of the disorders
v^{\bullet}(c)\in G^{\bullet}
and one of the spins
v^{\circ}(c)\in G^{\circ}
are chosen to be neighbors in
\Lambda(G)
, linked by an edge identified with a corner
c\in\Upsilon(G)
. In this case, one can formally denote the
fermion
at
c
by

\chi_{c}:=\mu_{v^{\bullet}(c)}\sigma_{v^{\circ}(c)},

(2.4)

One can now use (
2.3
) to construct the
Kadanoff-Ceva fermion
, in a purely combinatorial manner, setting

X_{\varpi}(c):=\mathbb{E}[\,\chi_{c}\mu_{v_{1}^{\bullet}}\ldots\mu_{v_{m-1}^{\bullet}}\sigma_{v_{1}^{\circ}}\ldots\sigma_{v_{n-1}^{\circ}}].

(2.5)

Given the above remarks, the fermionic observable
X_{\varpi}(c)
is defined up to a sign, but its definition becomes fully legitimate when working in
\Upsilon^{\times}_{\varpi}(G)
. Around
each
quad
z=(v_{0}^{\bullet},v_{0}^{\circ},v_{1}^{\bullet},v_{1}^{\circ})
(listing its vertices in counterclockwise order, as in
[
15
, Figure 3.A]
or Figure
1
), the Kadanoff-Ceva observables satisfy simple local linear equations, with coefficients depending only on the Ising coupling constant attached to the quad
z
. This propagation equation was first introduced in the works of
[
33
]
,
[
77
]
, and
[
67
, Section 4.3]
.

Let us be more concrete. Let
\theta_{z}
be the abstract angle corresponding to the parametrization in (
1.2
) of the edge in
G^{\bullet}
attached to the quad
z
. Then, for any triplet of corners
c_{pq}
(identified as
c_{pq}=(v^{\bullet}_{p}v^{\circ}_{q})
), where the lifts of
c_{pq}
,
c_{p,1-q}
, and
c_{1-p,q}
to
\Upsilon^{\times}_{\varpi}(G)
are neighbors, one has

X(c_{pq})=X(c_{p,1-q})\cos\theta_{z}+X(c_{1-p,q})\sin\theta_{z},

(2.6)

where . One can easily show that solutions to (
2.6
) are automatically spinors on
\Upsilon^{\times}_{\varpi}(G)
.

To conclude this overview of Kadanoff-Ceva correlators, we recall the generalization of the Dirac spinor
\eta_{c}
, which represents a special solution to the equation (
2.6
)
on isoradial grids
. Given a fixed embedding
\mathcal{S}:\Lambda(G)\to\mathbb{C}
of
\Lambda(G)
into the complex plane, we define (as in
[
27
]
):

\eta_{c}:=\varsigma\cdot\exp\big[-\tfrac{i}{2}\arg(\mathcal{S}(v^{\bullet}(c))-\mathcal{S}(v^{\circ}(c)))\big],\qquad\varsigma:=e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}},

(2.7)

where the prefactor
\varsigma=e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}}
is chosen for convenience. To avoid the sign ambiguity in (
2.7
), one can again work on the double cover
\Upsilon^{\times}(G)
. Specifically, the products
\eta_{c}X_{\varpi}(c):\Upsilon_{\varpi}(G)\to\mathbb{C}
are defined on
\Upsilon_{\varpi}(G)
, which only branches over
\varpi
. Note that we continue to use the notation in (
2.7
) below, even when
\mathcal{S}
is not an isoradial grid.

2.2.
Definition of an s-embeddings

We now present the embedding procedure introduced by Chelkak in
[
16
, Section 6]
and further developed in greater detail in
[
15
]
. First, we recall the definition of an s-embedding as given in
[
15
, Definition 2.1]
, which utilizes the Kadanoff-Ceva formalism. The general philosophy here is not based upon finding which weights are naturally attached to a given tilling of the plane by tangential quadrilateral but goes other way around, looking for an embedding that fits the Ising weights. The central idea is to use a solution to (
2.6
) in order to construct a concrete embedding associated with the weighted graph.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
(G,x)
be a weighted planar graph with the combinatorics of the plane, and let
\mathcal{X}:\Upsilon^{\times}(G)\to\mathbb{C}
be a solution to the full system of equations (
2.6
) around each quad. We say that
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}}:\Lambda(G)\to\mathbb{C}
is an s-embedding of
(G,x)
associated with
\mathcal{X}
if, for each
c\in\Upsilon^{\times}(G)
, we have

\mathcal{S}(v^{\bullet}(c))-\mathcal{S}(v^{\circ}(c))=(\mathcal{X}(c))^{2}.

(2.8)

For
z\in\diamondsuit(G)
, the quadrilateral
\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}(z)\subset\mathbb{C}
is the region bounded by the edges connecting the vertices
\mathcal{S}(v_{0}^{\bullet}(z))
,
\mathcal{S}(v_{0}^{\circ}(z))
,
\mathcal{S}(v_{1}^{\bullet}(z))
, and
\mathcal{S}(v_{1}^{\circ}(z))
. The s-embedding
\mathcal{S}
is called
proper
if the quadrilaterals
\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}(z)=(\mathcal{S}(v_{0}^{\bullet}(z))\mathcal{S}(v_{0}^{\circ}(z))\mathcal{S}(v_{1}^{\bullet}(z))\mathcal{S}(v_{1}^{\circ}(z)))
do not overlap. It is called
non-degenerate
if no quadrilateral
\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}(z)
degenerates into a segment. No convexity condition is imposed on the quadrilaterals
\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}(z)
.

Given a fixed solution
\mathcal{X}
to (
2.6
), it is not clear at all that the obtained embedding
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}}
is proper, meaning that finding a solution to (
2.6
) that leads to a non-degenerate proper picture is a non-trivial step. This will be one of the main innovations of the present paper. Starting from a proper s-embedding for a given set of weights, it will be possible to construct a proper s-embedding for another set a weights in a differential manner. One can also extend the definition of
\mathcal{S}
fixing the position of centers of quads
\diamondsuit(G)
, setting as in
[
15
, Equation (2.5)]

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{S}(v_{p}^{\bullet}(z))-\mathcal{S}(z):=\mathcal{X}(c_{p0})\mathcal{X}(c_{p1})\cos\theta_{z},\\[2.0pt]
\mathcal{S}(v_{q}^{\circ}(z))-\mathcal{S}(z):=-\mathcal{X}(c_{0q})\mathcal{X}(c_{1q})\sin\theta_{z},\end{array}

(2.9)

where
c_{p0}
and
c_{p1}
(respectively,
c_{0q}
and
c_{1q}
) are neighbors on
\Upsilon^{\times}(G)
.
The propagation equation (
2.6
) directly implies the consistency of both (
2.8
) and (
2.9
). From a more concrete perspective (see Figure (
1
)), the image
\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}(z)\in\mathbb{C}
of a combinatorial quadrilateral
z\in\diamondsuit(G)
, under the embedding
\mathcal{S}
, is a quadrilateral
tangent to a circle
centred at
\mathcal{S}(z)
defined in (
2.9
). The position of the point
\mathcal{S}(z)
corresponds to the intersection of the four bisectors of the sides of the tangential quadrilateral
\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}(z)
. The radius
r_{z}
of this circle can be determined using the values of
\mathcal{X}
, for instance, through
[
15
, Equation (2.7)]
. Let
\phi_{v,z}
represent the half-angle of the quadrilateral
\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}(z)
at
\mathcal{S}(v)
. It is then possible to reconstruct the abstract Ising weight
\theta_{z}
(in the parametrization (
1.2
)) from the angles in the image of
\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}(z)\subset\mathbb{C}
using the formula
[
15
, Equation (2.8)]
.

\tan\theta_{z}\ =\ \biggl(\frac{\sin\phi_{v_{0}^{\bullet},z}\sin\phi_{v_{1}^{\bullet},z}}{\sin\phi_{v_{0}^{\circ},z}\sin\phi_{v_{1}^{\circ},z}}\biggr)^{\!1/2}.

(2.10)

In the
s
-embedding framework, the large-scale properties of the origami map associated with an embedding determine whether a planar graph equipped with Ising weights can be interpreted as a (near)-critical system. The definition of this framework is recalled from
[
15
, Definition 2.2]
(see also
[
57
,
23
]
for the general definition in the dimer context).

Definition 2.2
.

Given
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}}
, the
origami
function, denoted by
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{X}}:\Lambda(G)\to\mathbb{R}
, is defined (up to a global additive constant) as a real-valued function. Its increments between two neighboring vertices
v^{\bullet}(c)
and
v^{\circ}(c)
are given by

\mathcal{Q}(v^{\bullet}(c))-\mathcal{Q}(v^{\circ}(c))\ :=\ |\mathcal{X}(c)|^{2}\,=\,|\mathcal{S}(v^{\bullet}(c))-\mathcal{S}(v^{\circ}(c))|\,.

(2.11)

We will often shorten
|\mathcal{X}(c)|^{2}=\delta_{c}
the length of the edge of
\Lambda(G)
attached to the corner
c
. The alternate sum of edge-lengths in a tangential quad vanishes, which ensures the consistent definition for
\mathcal{Q}
. One can see
\mathcal{Q}
as a folding of the quadrilaterals along their diagonals (see e.g.
[
23
, Section 8.2]
), which makes
\mathcal{Q}
a
1
-Lipschitz in the
\mathcal{S}
plane.

In a general tiling made by tangential quadrilaterals, especially one which is locally very irregular, one should first define some notion of
scale
before talking about large scale properties. We follow here the route taken in
[
23
]
, using the assumption
Lip(
\kappa
,
\delta
)
on
\mathcal{Q}
.

Assumption 2.1

(
Lip(
\kappa
,
\delta
)
)
.

We say that
\mathcal{S}
satisfies the assumption
\textup{Lip}(\kappa,\delta)
for some
0\leq\kappa<1
and
\delta>0
if for any
v,v^{\prime}
vertices of
\Lambda(G)

|\mathcal{Q}(v^{\prime})-\mathcal{Q}(v)|\leq\kappa\cdot|\mathcal{S}(v^{\prime})-\mathcal{S}(v)|\quad\text{if}\quad|\mathcal{S}(v^{\prime})-\mathcal{S}(v)|\geq\delta.

(2.12)

With the above assumption, one can define the scale of
\mathcal{S}
for the constant
\kappa<1
.

Definition 2.3
.

We say that an s-embedding
\mathcal{S}
covering the open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{C}
has a scale
\delta
for the constant
\kappa<1
on
U
if

\delta=\delta^{\kappa}:=\inf\{\tilde{\delta}>0,\textrm{Lip}(\kappa,\tilde{\delta})\textrm{ holds}\}.

(2.13)

In that case, one writes
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}^{\delta}=\mathcal{S}^{\delta^{\kappa}}
(removing the
\kappa
dependency in notations). In words, the scale of
\mathcal{S}
relative to the constant
\kappa
is the distance at which
\mathcal{Q}
becomes a
\kappa
-Lipschitz function. In the present paper, all constants
O()
are uniform on grids such that
\kappa<1
and can be in principle written explicitly using following the proofs inside
[
23
,
15
]
.

2.3.
S-holomorphic functions and associated primitives

Let us now recall the notion of
s-holomorphic functions
, which was generalized to
s
-embeddings in
[
15
]
. This concept was originally introduced by Smirnov
[
84
, Definition 3.1]
for the critical square lattice and by Chelkak and Smirnov
[
27
, Definition 3.1]
for isoradial grids. It plays a crucial role in applying discrete complex analysis techniques to the Ising and dimer models. We recall the general definition of
s
-holomorphic functions as stated in
[
15
, Definition 2.4]
. In what follows,
\textrm{Proj}[\cdot,\eta\mathbb{R}]
denotes the usual projection onto the line
\eta\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 2.4
.

A function
F
defined on a subset of
\diamondsuit(G)
is called
s
-holomorphic if, for each pair of adjacent quads
z,z’\in\diamondsuit(G)
separated by the edge
[\mathcal{S}(v^{\circ}(c));\mathcal{S}(v^{\bullet}(c))]
of
\mathcal{S}
attached to the corner
c
, we have

\textrm{Pr}[F(z),\eta_{c}\mathbb{R}]=\textrm{Pr}[F(z’),\eta_{c}\mathbb{R}].

(2.14)

The above definition establishes a direct link between real-valued solutions to (
2.6
) and complex-valued
s
-holomorphic functions. This connection was first presented in
[
15
, Proposition 2.5]
and in
[
23
, Appendix]
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}}
be a proper
s
-embedding and
F
an
s
-holomorphic on
\diamondsuit(G)
. Given
z\in\diamondsuit(G)
, a corner
c\in\Upsilon^{\times}(G)
belonging to the quad
z
, one can define the spinor
X
at
c\in\Upsilon^{\times}
by the formula

\displaystyle X(c)\

\displaystyle:=\ |\mathcal{S}(v^{\bullet}(c))-\mathcal{S}(v^{\circ}(c))|^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\operatorname{Re}[\overline{\eta}_{c}F(z)]

\displaystyle=\ \operatorname{Re}[\overline{\varsigma}\mathcal{X}(c)\cdot F(z)]\ =\ \overline{\varsigma}\mathcal{X}(c)\cdot\mathrm{Proj}[F(z);\eta_{c}\mathbb{R}].

(2.15)

The map
c\mapsto X(c)
satisfies all three terms identities (
2.6
) around the quad
z
. Conversely given
X:\Upsilon^{\times}(G)\to\mathbb{R}
a real valued solution to (
2.6
), there exists a unique s-holomorphic function
F
on
\diamondsuit(G)
such that the identity (
2.15
) holds.

When
F
and
X
are linked by (
2.15
), one can reconstruct the value of
F(z)
out of the values of
X
at any pair of corners
c_{pq}(z)\in\Upsilon^{\times}(G)
and the geometry of
\mathcal{S}
, e.g. using the formula
[
15
, Corollary 2.6]

F(z)\ =\ -i\varsigma\cdot\frac{\overline{\mathcal{X}(c_{01}(z))}\,X(c_{10}(z))-\overline{\mathcal{X}(c_{10}(z))}\,X(c_{01}(z))}{\operatorname{Im}[\,\overline{\mathcal{X}(c_{01}(z))}\,\mathcal{X}(c_{10}(z))}.

(2.16)

In the
s
-embeddings framework, the behavior of the scaling limit of
s
-holomorphic functions is determined by its local equation and boundary conditions. This can be understood through two different integration procedures at the discrete level. The first is a standard extension of the integration procedure for discrete holomorphic functions to the
s
-embeddings framework, which now accounts for the presence of the origami map. The second is a generalization of Smirnov’s primitive of the square of an
s
-holomorphic function. The former is extensively studied in
[
23
, Proposition 6.15]
and will be useful for deriving the local regularity theory for discrete functions, along with their local equation in the limit. The latter, introduced by Smirnov in
[
84
]
for the critical square lattice, helps identify a discrete Riemann-Hilbert boundary condition that arises in the Ising context. Let us begin with the primitive
I_{\mathbb{C}}
. Given an
s
-holomorphic function
F
on
\diamondsuit(G)
, we can define (up to a global additive constant)

[
15
, Section 2.3]

I_{\mathbb{C}}[F]:=\int\big(\overline{\varsigma}Fd\mathcal{S}+\varsigma\overline{F}d\mathcal{Q}\big).

(2.17)

Let
v_{1,2}^{\bullet},v_{1,2}^{\circ}
be vertices of the quad
z\in\diamondsuit(G)
. Then one has for
\star\in\{\bullet,\circ\}

I_{\mathbb{C}}[F](v_{2}^{\star})-I_{\mathbb{C}}[F](v_{1}^{\star})=\overline{\varsigma}F(z)[\mathcal{S}(v_{2}^{\star})-\mathcal{S}(v_{1}^{\star})]+\varsigma\overline{F(z)}[\mathcal{Q}(v_{2}^{\star})-\mathcal{Q}(v_{1}^{\star})].

(2.18)

It is possible to extend to origami map to the entire complex plane (see
[
23
]
,
[
15
, Section 2.3]
or
[
62
, Section 2.4]
) which allows to extend the definition (
2.17
) to the entire complex plane.

The notion of the primitive of the square
H_{X}
can be introduced through a purely combinatorial definition, linked to the Kadanoff-Ceva formalism. This definition does not require any specific embedding into the plane, as long as one works with a spinor
X
that satisfies (
2.6
). This generalization of Smirnov’s original work is presented in
[
15
, Definition 2.8]
.

Definition 2.6
.

Given
X
a spinor on
\Upsilon^{\times}(G)
satisfying (
2.6
), one can define the function
H_{X}
up to a global additive constant on
\Lambda(G)\cup\diamondsuit(G)
by setting

\begin{array}[]{rcll}H_{X}(v^{\bullet}_{p}(z))-H_{X}(z)&:=&X(c_{p0}(z))X(c_{p1}(z))\cos\theta_{z},&p=0,1,\\[2.0pt]
H_{X}(v^{\circ}_{q}(z))-H_{X}(z)&:=&-X(c_{0q}(z))X(c_{1q}(z))\sin\theta_{z},&q=0,1,\\[2.0pt]
H_{X}(v^{\bullet}_{p}(z))-H_{X}(v^{\circ}_{q}(z))&:=&(X(c_{pq}(z)))^{2},\end{array}

(2.19)

similarly to (
2.8
) and (
2.9
).

The consistency of the above definition follows from (
2.6
). When passing to an
s
-embedding
\mathcal{S}
of the graph
(G,x)
, the correspondence between
X
and
F
, as recalled in Proposition
2.5
, allows us to interpret
H_{X}
via the
s
-holomorphic function
F
associated with
X
. More precisely, one can define
H_{X}
as in
[
15
, Equation (2.17)]
.

H_{F}:=\int\operatorname{Re}(\overline{\varsigma}^{2}F^{2}d\mathcal{S}+|F|^{2}d\mathcal{Q})=\int(\operatorname{Im}(F^{2}d\mathcal{S})+\operatorname{Re}(|F|^{2}d\mathcal{Q})),

(2.20)

on
\Lambda(G)\cup\diamondsuit(G)
. The extension of
\mathcal{Q}
described below allows us to extend
H_{F}
in a piecewise affine manner to the entire plane. (It is important to note that the extension occurs on each face of the associated
t
-embedding
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{S}
— see
[
23
, Proposition 3.10]
.) The following lemma establishes the relationship between the definitions (
2.19
) and (
2.20
), proving that they are, in fact, the
same
function.

Lemma 2.7
.

[
15
, Lemma 2.9]
Let
F
be defined
\diamondsuit(G)
and
X
be defined on
\Upsilon^{\times}(G)
related by the identity (
2.15
). Then, the functions
H_{F}
and
H_{X}
coincide up to a global additive constant.

If
\mathcal{S}
is an isoradial grid, the origami map
\mathcal{Q}
is constant on both
G^{\bullet}
and
G^{\circ}
(since all the edges of the quads
\mathcal{S}^{\diamond}(z)
have the same length). Therefore,
H_{F}
is the primitive of
\operatorname{Im}[F^{2}d\mathcal{S}]
, which recovers the original definition given in
[
27
, Section 3.3]
.

2.4.
Regularity theory for s-holomorphic functions

In this short subsection we recall in a concise way the regularity theory of s-holomorphic functions, which was developed in the dimer context in
[
23
, Section 6.5]
and the Ising context in
[
15
, Theorem 2.18]
. That regularity theory can be summarized as some Harnack type inequality for s-holomorphic functions that controls its maximum via one of its primitives
I_{\mathbb{C}}[F]
or
H_{F}
defined in the previous section, except in some pathological scenario where the discrete functions blow up exponentially fast in
\delta^{-1}
.

Theorem 2.1
.

[
23
, Theorem 6.17]
and
[
15
, Theorem 2.18]
For each fixed
\kappa<1
, there exist constants
\gamma_{0}=\gamma_{0}(\kappa)>0
and
C_{0}=C_{0}(\kappa)>0
such that the following alternative holds. Let
F
be a s-holomorphic function defined in a ball of radius
r
drawn over an s-embedding
\mathcal{S}
satisfying the assumption
\textup{Lip}(\kappa,\delta)
and some
\alpha\in\mathbb{T}
. Then, provided that
r\geq\operatorname{cst}\cdot\delta
for a constant depending only on
\kappa
, one has the two following alternatives:

For the integration procedure (
2.17
)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\text{either}\ \max_{\{z:\mathcal{S}(z)\in B(u,\frac{1}{2}r)\}}|F|&\leq&C_{0}r^{-1}\cdot|\operatorname{osc}_{\{v:\mathcal{S}(v)\in B(u,r)\}}I_{\alpha\mathbb{R}}[\overline{\varsigma}F]|,\\[4.0pt]
\text{or}\ \max_{\{z:\mathcal{S}(z)\in B(u,\frac{3}{4}r)\}}|F|&\geq&\exp(\gamma_{0}r\delta^{-1})\cdot C_{0}r^{-1}|\operatorname{osc}_{\{v:\mathcal{S}(v)\in B(u,r)\}}I_{\alpha\mathbb{R}}[\overline{\varsigma}F]|\end{array}

For the integration procedure (
2.20
)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\text{either}\ \max_{\{z:\mathcal{S}(z)\in B(u,\frac{1}{2}r)\}}|F|^{2}&\leq&C_{0}r^{-1}\cdot|\operatorname{osc}_{\{v:\mathcal{S}(v)\in B(u,r)\}}H_{F}|,\\[4.0pt]
\text{or}\ \max_{\{z:\mathcal{S}(z)\in B(u,\frac{3}{4}r)\}}|F|^{2}&\geq&\exp(\gamma_{0}r\delta^{-1})\cdot C_{0}r^{-1}|\operatorname{osc}_{\{v:\mathcal{S}(v)\in B(u,r)\}}H_{F}|\end{array}

Under the assumption
Unif(
\delta
)
, which is a the simplest toy example where the couple of assumptions
Lip(
\kappa
,
\delta
)
and
Exp-Fat(
\delta
,
\rho(\delta)
)
hold, the second alternative of the theorems recalled above are impossible. In particular, this ensures that (see e.g.
[
15
, Remark 2.12]
) that if the s-holomorphic functions
F^{\delta}
are uniformly bounded in an open set
U
, they form a pre-compact family (in the topology of the uniform convergence on compacts) as
\delta\to 0
. In particular, functions are
\beta(\kappa)
-Hölder (see
[
15
, Theorem 2.18]
) starting at a scale comparable to
\delta
. More generally, under the general assumptions
Lip(
\kappa
,
\delta
)
and
Exp-Fat(
\delta
,
\rho(\delta)
)
, bounded s-holomorphic functions are
\beta(\kappa)
-Hölder starting at a scale
O(\max(\delta,\rho(\delta))
.

2.5.
Constructing full-plane fermions and identifying their branching structure

Let us now construct the full-plane energy Kadanoff-Ceva correlator as the limit of the same object in bounded regions. First, fix a connected box
\Lambda_{R}
and consider the Ising model on
\Lambda_{R}
with wired boundary conditions, meaning that a single spin is attached to the outer face. Fix a corner
q\in\Upsilon
, whose two lifts in
\Upsilon^{\times}
are denoted by
q^{+}
and
q^{-}
. We denote by
u^{\circ}(q)\in G^{\circ}
and
v^{\bullet}(q)\in G^{\bullet}
the vertices of
\Lambda(G)
adjacent to
q
. Now, using the formalism introduced in (
2.5
) with
\varpi={u^{\circ}(q),v^{\bullet}(q)}
, we define

X_{R}^{(q)}(c)=\langle\chi_{c}\chi_{q}\rangle^{\textrm{(w)}}_{\Lambda_{R}}:=\mathbb{E}^{\textrm{(w)}}\big[\sigma_{u^{\circ}(c)}\sigma_{u^{\circ}(q)}\mu_{v^{\bullet}(c)}\mu_{v^{\bullet}(q)}\big].

(2.21)

The correlator
c\mapsto X_{R}^{(q)}(c)
is a spinor that satisfies the propagation equation (
2.6
)
everywhere
in
\Upsilon^{\times}{(q)}:=\Upsilon^{\times}{[u^{\circ}(q),v^{\bullet}(q)]}
, the double cover
\Upsilon^{\times}_{(q)}
that branches everywhere except around
u^{\circ}(q)
and
v^{\bullet}(q)
. As recalled in
[
21
, Figure 6]
(see also Figure
2
), it is possible to identify the two double covers
\Upsilon^{\times}
and
\Upsilon^{\times}_{(q)}
, except at the corner
q
, where the nearby connections to
q^{\pm}
must be swapped on one of the quads containing the edge
[u^{\circ}(q),v^{\bullet}(q)]
.

More precisely, the corner
q^{+}\in\Upsilon^{\times}{(q)}
is chosen so that the branching structures of
\Upsilon^{\times}{(q)}
and
\Upsilon^{\times}
coincide around the quad
z^{+}_{q}
. In particular, when looking the correlator
X^{(q)}_{R}
as a function on the double-cover
\Upsilon^{\times}
, it satisfies the propagation equation around almost all quads, including
z_{q}^{+}
, but in general the propagation equations around the quad
z_{q}^{-}
. More precisely, around the quad
z_{q}^{-}
, each identity of the form (
2.6
) involving the corners
q^{\pm}
fails,
while it would be true if one had assigned the values

X^{(q)}_{R}(q^{\pm})=\mp 1

instead of the true values

X^{(q)}_{R}(q^{\pm})=\pm 1
. This rather simple observation is the building block of the entire paper and will be used to deform continuously embeddings while changing continuously Ising weights.

It is standard to consider a full-plane Ising model, obtained as a subsequential limit of models on an increasing sequence of bounded domains, for a weighted planar graph
(S,(x_{e}))_{e\in E}
. We also fix an arbitrary embedding
S
of
(S,(x_{e}))_{e\in E}
into the plane, which is defined up to homeomorphism. Recall that, in general, for any corner
c\in\Upsilon
, the disorder random variable can be written as
\mu_{v^{\bullet}(c)}\mu_{v^{\bullet}(q)}=\prod_{e\in\gamma^{\bullet}_{(q,c)}}x_{e}^{\varepsilon_{e}}
, where the product is taken along a disorder line
\gamma^{\bullet}_{(q,c)}
connecting
v^{\bullet}(c)
to
v^{\bullet}(q)
, and
\varepsilon_{e}
represents the energy density at edge
e
. Using the identity
x_{e}^{\varepsilon_{e}}=\frac{1}{2}\big((x_{e}-x_{e}^{-1})\varepsilon_{e}+(x_{e}+x_{e}^{-1}))
, one can expand the bounded correlator
X_{R}^{(a)}
, following
[
21
, Lemma 4.1]
, as:

X_{R}^{(q)}(c)=\sum_{\iota\in I(q,c)}a_{\iota}\mathbb{E}^{(\textrm{w})}_{\Lambda_{R}}[\sigma_{A_{\iota}}],

(2.22)

where the sum is finite linear combination of spin-correlations where the products
\sigma_{A_{\iota}}=\prod_{j\in\iota}\sigma_{j}
, only involve spins along the disorder line
\gamma^{\bullet}_{(q,c)}
. In particular, the indices
\iota\in I(q,c)
do not depend on
R
(provided that
R
is chosen sufficiently large), and the sum contains only a bounded number of terms as
R\to\infty
. Since
\mathbb{E}^{(\textrm{w})}_{\Lambda_{R}}[\sigma_{A_{\iota}}]
is decreasing as
R\to\infty
, the expression
X_{R}^{(q)}(c)
has a well-defined limit
X_{S}^{(q)}(c)
as
R
becomes infinite. In the present paper, the strong box-crossing property recalled in Theorem
1.2
holds at a large enough scale for all s-embedding we work with. This ensures the uniqueness of the full-plane Gibbs measure for all Ising model studied here and makes the constructions of the fermion
X_{S}^{(q)}
independent from the wired boundary conditions used in bounded regions.

Figure 2
.
(Left) The double cover
\Upsilon^{\times}
branching around each vertex of
G^{\bullet}\cup G^{\circ}\cup\diamondsuit(G)
(Right) The double cover
\Upsilon^{\times}_{(q)}
that branches everywhere except around
v^{\bullet}(q)
and
u^{\circ}(q)
. Those two double covers can be identified with each other away from
q
. The corner
q^{+}
is chosen so that the two double-covers have the same branching structure around the quad
z_{q}^{+}
. This figure is similar to
[
21
, Figure 6]
.

2.6.
Rate of growth of full-plane fermions via the geometry of the embedding

The key ingredient to understand in a quantitative fashion the impact of the deformation process via the ODE (
3.22
) or the SDE (
5.1
) is to get some quantitative estimates regarding the decay of two points fermion
\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}\rangle_{S}
associated to the Ising model on
(S,(x_{e})_{e\in S})
. The main Theorem of
[
62
]
together with its proof ensures that one can obtain sharp (up to constant) estimates on the decay of the two-points fermions via the geometry of an associated proper
s
-embedding
\mathcal{S}
. In the case of a grid satisfying
Unif(
\delta
)
there exists
\Theta
, only depending on
r_{0},\theta_{0}
such that for any pair of corners
\mathfrak{p}\neq\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
, one has

|\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}}\rangle_{S}|\leq|\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathfrak{p})|\cdot|\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})|\cdot\frac{\Theta}{|\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{p})-\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})|}.

(2.23)

This observation is a direct consequence of the similar estimate when
|\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{p})-\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})|\geq C_{0}\cdot\delta
, obtained for the associated complexified fermion within the proof of
[
62
, Theorem 1.2]
. Moreover, since all the angles in
\mathcal{S}
are bounded from below by
\theta_{0}
, the formula (
2.10
) ensures that all coupling constants in a grid satisfying
Unif(
\delta
)
are bounded away from
0
and
1
, again depending only on
r_{0},\theta_{0}
. Using the finite energy property, this allows to extend (
2.23
) even in the case where
|\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{p})-\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{p}^{\prime})|\leq C_{0}\cdot\delta
.

2.7.
The argument principle to prove properness of the embedding

The deformation process presented in Section
3
is purely algebraic and doesn’t provide any statement regarding properness of the deformed embedding. One of the main outputs of the present paper is the construction the
s
-embeddings
(\mathcal{S}^{(t)})_{t\geq 0}
of massive and near-critical i.i.d. models in Sections
4
and
5
, via (
3.8
). Still, if one wants to apply embeddings techniques to deduce statements about the associated Ising models, one should check that all embeddings
(\mathcal{S}^{(t)})_{t\geq 0}
remain
proper
, at least for small enough times
t
. We explain here how one can easily check if an
s
-embedding is locally proper, meaning that all faces
do not overlap
. Properness is a priori a non-trivial statement to deduce looking only at the generator
\mathcal{Y}
associated to an s-embedding
\mathcal{S}
. More importantly, it looks to be a very local statement that should be checked for any pair of different faces. Fortunately, there exists a standard argument principle that allows to rewrite this very local statement as a more global one, counting the winding number of a concrete curve. The argument principle we present here is very specific to the use of Ising/dimers embeddings, as its proof is based on harmonicity of
s/t
-holomorphic functions on the so-called S-graphs (see e.g.
[
23
, Section 4]
or
[
15
, Section 2.3]
for more details.

We recall here simple facts coming from
[
22
, Section 4.1]
and limit ourselves to an
s
-embedding the square lattice to simplify the presentation. All statements presented below apply verbatim in a more general context. Denote the
combinatorial
box
(\Lambda^{\textrm{comb}}_{n},(x_{e})_{e\in\Lambda_{n}})
of size
2n
centred at the origin, equipped with the Ising weights
(x_{e})_{e\in\Lambda_{n}})
. We denote by
S
its standard embedding on the square lattice (meaning here that Ising weights
don’t
have the interpretation (
2.10
). Denote by
\gamma^{S}_{n}
the outer-boundary of
\Lambda^{\textrm{comb}}_{n}
oriented in the positive direction, which is a simple curve. Let
\mathcal{S}
be an s-embedding of
(\Lambda^{\textrm{comb}}_{2n},(x_{e})_{e\in\Lambda_{2n}})
. The argument principle, recalled in
[
22
, Section 4.1]
, reads as follows:

If the path
\mathcal{S}(\gamma^{S}_{2n})
, oriented in the positive direction (which is not a priori a simple curve), only winds
once
around the image of
\mathcal{S}(\gamma^{S}_{n})
, then
\mathcal{S}(\Lambda^{\textrm{comb}}_{n})
is a proper
s
-embedding.

This comes as a byproduct of the following facts:

•

All the faces of
\mathcal{S}(\Lambda^{\textrm{comb}}_{2n})
are oriented in the same direction. This is a consequence of the maximum-principle for harmonic functions on T-graphs
[
23
, Section 4]
together with the fact that the image of each face
f\in\Lambda^{\textrm{abs}}_{2n}
of the associated
t
-embedding is mapped to a convex polygon.

•

The images of two different faces
f_{1}\neq f_{2}\in\Lambda^{\textrm{abs}}_{n}
don’t overlap. Either way, if the faces
f_{1},f_{2}
overlap around an interior point
z\in\mathcal{S}(\Lambda^{\textrm{comb}}_{n})
, then one has

1=\textrm{wind}(\mathcal{S}(\gamma^{S}_{2n}),z)=\sum\limits_{f\in\Lambda^{\textrm{comb}}_{2n}}\textrm{wind}(\mathcal{S}(f),z)\geq\textrm{wind}(\mathcal{S}(f_{1}),z)+\textrm{wind}(\mathcal{S}(f_{2}),z)\geq 2,

as
\mathcal{S}(\gamma^{S}_{2n})
only winds once around
z
(first equality) and the orientation of all faces are the same, which implies that
\textrm{wind}(\mathcal{S}(f),z)\geq 0
for all faces of
f\in\Lambda_{2n}
.

Let us additionally the for each face
f
, its image
\mathcal{S}(f)
is a tangential quadrilateral, whose sum of opposing edge-length are equal. Therefore, it is straightforward that the images of the boundary segments of the face
f

cannot
intersect except at the image of the vertices of
f
.

3.
Deforming an s-embedding using differential equations

The goal of this section is to give some rigorous meaning to the overall philosophy of the present paper. Assume one starts with a given s-embedding
\mathcal{S}
of a graph
(S,(x_{e})_{e\in E})
. It is tempting to say that when one moves continuously the family of Ising weights
(x^{(t)}_{e})_{e\in E}
with some continuous time parameter
t
, one should be able to construct a continuous family
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}
of
s
-embeddings of the corresponding Ising model
(S,(x^{(t)}_{e})_{e\in E})
. This construction is a priori non-trivial, as finding an
s
-embedding
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}
requires finding a vector in the kernel of some linear system given by the Ising weights
(x^{(t)}_{e})_{e\in E}
. It is not clear that the kernel becomes non-degenerate as time evolves, and even less clear that one can find a vector in the kernel in a continuous manner. To simplify the understanding, we work in the section in the case where
S
is the square lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, equipped with a given set of weights
(x_{e})_{e\in G}
. Still, the whole reasoning applies verbatim to general
s
-embeddings. The corners of the square lattice can be splitted into
4
subsets, depending on their ’geographic’ position around vertical edges. Those relative position are denoted respectively - with the natural geographic interpretation- ’north-east’ (NE), ’north-west’ (NW), ’south-east’ (SE) and ’south-west’ (SW). In what follows, the SE corners are labeled with the letter
a
, the NE corners are labeled with the letter
b
, the, the NW corners are labeled with the letter
c
and the SW corners are labeled with the letter
d
.

3.1.
Modifying one Ising weight using Kadanoff-Ceva mismatches

Let us start with the simplest possible deformation, aiming to construct a new s-embedding
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
of a weighted graph
(S,(\hat{x}_{e})_{e\in E})

\displaystyle\times\begin{bmatrix}y_{a_{0}}\\

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}-\mathcal{Y}(a_{0}^{+})+\mathcal{Y}(d_{0}^{+})\cos\hat{\theta}_{0}-\mathcal{Y}(b_{0}^{+})\sin\hat{\theta}_{0}\\

\mathcal{Y}(b_{0}^{+})
and
\mathcal{Y}(d_{0}^{+})
terms in
V
only involving
\mathcal{Y}(a_{0}^{+})
and
\mathcal{Y}(c_{0}^{+})
and trigonometric functions, which reads as

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=2\sin\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}\begin{bmatrix}\mathcal{Y}(a_{0}^{+})\sin\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}+\mathcal{Y}(c_{0}^{+})\cos\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}\\
\mathcal{Y}(a_{0}^{+})\cos\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}+\theta_{0}}{2}+\mathcal{Y}(c_{0}^{+})\sin\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}+\theta_{0}}{2}\\
-\mathcal{Y}(a_{0}^{+})\cos\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}+\mathcal{Y}(c_{0}^{+})\sin\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}\\
\mathcal{Y}(a_{0}^{+})\sin\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}+\theta_{0}}{2}-\mathcal{Y}(c_{0}^{+})\cos\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}+\theta_{0}}{2}\end{bmatrix}

(3.4)

It is straightforward to see that choosing

\displaystyle y_{a_{0}}

\displaystyle:=\sin\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}\big(\mathcal{Y}(a_{0}^{+})\sin\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}+\mathcal{Y}(c_{0}^{+})\cos\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2})

\displaystyle y_{c_{0}}

\displaystyle:=\sin\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}\big(-\mathcal{Y}(a_{0}^{+})\cos\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}+\mathcal{Y}(c_{0}^{+})\sin\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}),

then
\mathcal{Y}+y_{a_{0}}\cdot X^{(a_{0})}_{\hat{S}}+y_{c_{0}}\cdot X^{(c_{0})}_{\hat{S}}
indeed satisfies (
2.6
) around the quad
z_{0}
for the coupling constant
\hat{x}_{0}=\tan\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}}{2}
. This sequence of rather simple observations are the building block of the deformation procedure, that we precise in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an
s
-embedding of
(S,(x_{e})_{e\in E})
associated to the propagator
\mathcal{Y}
. Consider the Ising model on
(S,(\hat{x}_{e})_{e\in E})
, where the set of coupling constant only differ at an edge
e_{0}
. Using the parametrisation (
1.2
), define the abstract angles
x_{e_{0}}=\tan\frac{\theta_{0}}{2}
and
\hat{x}_{e_{0}}=\tan\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}}{2}
. Following the identification of double covers made in Section
2.5
, fix a ’south-eastern’ corner
a_{0}^{+}\in\Upsilon^{\times}
such that the branching structures of the double-covers
\Upsilon^{\times}_{(a_{0})}
and
\Upsilon^{\times}
don’t coincide on
z_{0}
. Similarly, fix a ’north-western’ corner
c_{0}^{+}\in\Upsilon^{\times}
such that the branching structures of the double-covers
\Upsilon^{\times}_{(c_{0})}
and
\Upsilon^{\times}
don’t coincide on
z_{0}
. Let
X^{(a_{0})}_{\hat{S}}
(respectively
X^{(c_{0})}_{\hat{S}}
) the Kadanoff-Ceva fermions normalised such that
X^{(a_{0})}_{\hat{S}}(a_{0}^{+})=1
(respectively
X^{(c_{0})}_{\hat{S}}(c_{0}^{+})=1
). Then, as long as
y_{a_{0}}
and
y_{c_{0}}
are chosen as in (
3.5
),(
3.6
), then
\hat{\mathcal{Y}}:=\mathcal{Y}+y_{a_{0}}X^{(a_{0})}_{\hat{S}}+y_{c_{0}}X^{(c_{0})}_{\hat{S}}
is an s-embedding of
(S,(\hat{x}_{e})_{e\in E})
.

\displaystyle y_{a_{0}}

\displaystyle:=\sin\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}\Big(\mathcal{Y}(a_{0}^{+})\sin\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}+\mathcal{Y}(c_{0}^{+})\cos\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}\Big)

(3.5)

\displaystyle y_{c_{0}}

\displaystyle:=\sin\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}\Big(-\mathcal{Y}(a_{0}^{+})\cos\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}+\mathcal{Y}(c_{0}^{+})\sin\frac{\hat{\theta}_{0}-\theta_{0}}{2}\Big).

(3.6)

3.2.
Differential evolution of
s
-embeddings

One can in principle use the previous lemma in a recursive manner to change not-only one Ising weight but a finite collection of them. From that perspective, this naive recursive approach has several flaws. The first one is that the output of a recursive construction may depend on the order chosen to modify the edge weights. More importantly, one needs to have a refined understanding of the large scale behaviour of the fermions
X^{(a_{0})}_{\hat{S}}
to understand the deviation between the embeddings. The main result of
[
62
]
provides an excellent understanding of
X^{(a_{0})}_{S}
via the geometry of the associated
s
-embedding
\mathcal{S}
, with explicit scaling factors and a complete description of its continuous counterpart. As the original data is an
s
-embedding
\mathcal{S}
of
S
and not an
s
-embedding of
\hat{S}
, in order to get some precise understanding of the evolution of the embedding, one needs to get some additional estimates regarding the deviation between the combinatorial fermions
X^{(a_{0})}_{S}
and
X^{(a_{0})}_{\hat{S}}
. To avoid both issues, we take a more conceptual approach (first suggested by Dmitry Krachun) which consist in deforming a finite collection of weights in an infinitesimal manner with some time parameter
t
, obtaining the fermion
\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}
as a solution to a first order ODE. Beyond the possibility to use the very rich literature on differential equations, this approach constructs for free solutions to (
2.6
) when moving continuously the weights, solving (at least in the finite volume case) jumping over one of the main barriers in the embedding setup, which is finding a correct embedding before starting the overall analysis.

We describe now this continuous time deformation process, still working on the square lattice
S=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Once again, the following formalism works in full generality. Fix two collections
(a_{k})_{k\in\Lambda_{n}}
and
(c_{k})_{k\in\Lambda_{n}}
of SE and NW corners inside
\Lambda_{n}
, and choose respective lifts
(a^{+}_{k})_{k\in\Lambda_{n}}
and
(c^{+}_{k})_{k\in\Lambda_{n}}
as in Lemma
3.1
. For
t\geq 0
a small enough time parameter, consider the family of Ising models
(S,(x^{(t)}_{e})_{e\in E(\mathbb{Z}^{2}}))_{t\geq 0}
, where the angle in (
1.2
) for the edge
e\in E(\mathbb{Z}^{2})
is given by

\theta^{(t)}_{e}:=\theta^{(0)}_{e}+m_{e}\cdot t,

(3.7)

with a
mass
parameter
m_{e}
. In what follows, the only input is a given
s
-embedding
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
of
(S,(x^{(0)}_{e})_{e\in E(\mathbb{Z}^{2})})
of the model at the initial time
t=0
. The following lemma allows to construct a family of
s
-embedding of
(S(t))_{t\geq 0}=(S,(x^{(t)}_{e})_{e\in E(\mathbb{Z}^{2})})_{t\geq 0}
as a solution to some differential equation with initial condition
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
. For the rest of this section, one denotes generic corners with the letter
\mathfrak{p}
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}:=d\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
associated to
(S,(x^{(0)}_{e})_{e\in E(\mathbb{Z}^{2})})
. Assume also that the collection of masses
(m_{e})_{e\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
vanishes outside the box
\Lambda
. Consider the differential system defined on corners of
\Upsilon^{\times}\cap\Lambda
, whose initial condition is given by
\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}
and whose dynamic is given for any
\mathfrak{p}\in\Lambda
by

\frac{d\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p})}{dt}=\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{z_{k}\in\Lambda}m_{k}\Bigg[\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}-\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}\Bigg].

(3.8)

Then

(1)

There exist
T_{0}(\Lambda)>0
such that the ODE (
3.8
) is well defined on
[0;T_{0}(\Lambda)]
.

(2)

For any time
0\leq t\leq T_{0}(\Lambda)
, the fermion
\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}
solution to (
3.8
) constructs an s-embedding
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}
of the Ising model
S(t)
.

Before diving into the proof, let us detail the intuition leading to (
3.8
). Using the exact expression of the coefficients
y_{a_{k}}
and
y_{c_{k}}
in Lemma
3.1
when moving infinitesimally from one weight
\theta_{k}
to
\hat{\theta}_{k}
, one sees right away that

\frac{d\mathcal{Y}(\mathfrak{p})}{d\theta_{k}}=\frac{m_{k}}{2}\Bigg(\mathcal{Y}(c^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S}-\mathcal{Y}(a^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S}\Bigg),

(3.9)

as the correlators
\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S}
and
\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S}
are continuous with respect to
\theta_{k}
(the correlators involved here correspond to the Ising model with the original weights given by
\theta_{k}
) . In the case where one recursively moves infinitesimally all the coupling constants in
\Lambda
as
\theta^{(t)}_{j}=\theta_{j}+t\cdot m_{j}
for
t
small enough, one can extend the last observation and use the continuity of fermions with respect to
t
. Thus, there exist
s
-embedding
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}
of
(S,(x^{(t)}_{e})_{e\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}})
such that

\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p})=\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(\mathfrak{p})+\frac{t}{2}\sum\limits_{z_{k}\in\Lambda}m_{k}\Bigg[\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(c^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(0)}-\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(a^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(0)}\Bigg]+o(t),

(3.10)

which hints that (
3.8
) indeed constructs an appropriate
s
-embedding.

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

For a finite box
\Lambda
, the first order linear ODE (
3.8
) rewrites as
Y^{\prime}(t)=A(t)Y(t)
for some matrix
A(t)
and a finite dimensional vector
Y
. The
\mathcal{L}^{1}
norm of
A
is bounded by
O(\sum_{e_{k}\in\Lambda}|m_{e_{k}}|)
at any time as the fermionic correlators are trivially bounded by
1
. Therefore there exist
T_{0}(\Lambda)>0
such that the solution to (
3.8
) exists and is unique in
[0;T_{0}(\Lambda)]
. Let us now check that the solution to (
3.8
) at time
t
indeed satisfies all propagation identities (
2.6
) for the angles
(\theta_{e}^{(t)})_{e\in\Lambda}
. More concretely, one needs to check that for any corner
\mathfrak{p}
neighbouring
\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
in
\Upsilon^{\times}
in the quad
z_{j}\in\diamondsuit
, one has

\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p})=\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})+\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{-}).

(3.11)

Denote by
\mathcal{T}_{(t)}
the finite dimensional vector indexed by trios of neighboring corners
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\in\Upsilon^{\times}\cap\Lambda
around a quad
z_{j}
whose coordinates are given by

\mathcal{T}_{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}):=\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p})-\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})-\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{-}).

(3.12)

We are going to prove in
Steps 1-3
that
\mathcal{T}
satisfies some first order ODE of the form
(\mathcal{T}_{(t)})^{\prime}=B\times\mathcal{T}_{(t)}
for some fixed matrix
B
.

Step 1: Identifying cancellations for
\mathcal{T}_{(t)}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{p)}
:
by construction, the derivative of the LHS of (
3.11
) is given by the RHS of (
3.8
). The derivative of the RHS of (
3.11
) is given by the

m_{j}\Bigg[-\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})+\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{-})\Bigg]+\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})+\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{-}).

(3.13)

When computing
\mathcal{T}_{(t)}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{p})
at the coordinate corresponding to the trio
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}\in z_{j}
, it turns out that all terms involving quads
z_{k}\neq z_{j}
cancel, leaving only few terms to analyse. The goal of this first step is to identify those cancellations. For the last two terms in (
3.13
) involving
\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{\pm})
, one has

\displaystyle\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})+\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{-})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{z_{k}\in\Lambda}\frac{m_{k}}{2}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})

\displaystyle(\star_{1})

\displaystyle\quad-\sum\limits_{z_{k}\Lambda}\frac{m_{k}}{2}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\chi_{c^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})

\displaystyle(\star_{2})

\displaystyle\quad+\sum\limits_{z_{k}\in\Lambda}\frac{m_{k}}{2}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})

\displaystyle(\star_{3})

\displaystyle\quad-\sum\limits_{z_{k}\in\Lambda}\frac{m_{k}}{2}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}\chi_{c^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})

\displaystyle(\star_{4})

Fix
k\neq j
. The
combinatorial correlator

\mathfrak{q}\mapsto\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}
satisfies (
2.6
) on
\Upsilon^{\times}
around
z_{j}
, with respect to the angle
\theta^{(t)}_{j}
. In particular this ensures that

\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})+\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})=\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}.

(3.14)

One can then multiply both sides of (
3.14
) by
m_{k}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c^{+}_{k})
, and sum all equalities corresponding to quads
z_{k}\neq z_{j}
. This ensures that

\sum\limits_{\underset{k\neq j}{z_{k}\in\Lambda}}m_{k}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c^{+}_{k})\cdot\Bigg(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})+\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\Bigg)=\\
\sum\limits_{\underset{k\neq j}{z_{k}\in\Lambda}}m_{k}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}.

Omitting the contributions around the quad
z_{j}
containing
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\pm}
, the first line of the above equation corresponds to
(\star_{1}+\star_{3})
while the second line corresponds to the
\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c_{k}^{+})
terms in (
3.8
). Similarly one has,

\sum\limits_{\underset{k\neq j}{z_{k}\in\Lambda}}m_{k}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a^{+}_{k})\cdot\Bigg(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\chi_{c^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})+\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}\chi_{c^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\Bigg)=\\
\sum\limits_{\underset{k\neq j}{z_{k}\in\Lambda}}m_{k}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a_{k}^{+})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)},

where omitting once again the contributions around the quad
z_{j}
, the first line of the above equation corresponds to
(\star_{2}+\star_{4})
while the second line corresponds to the
\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a_{k}^{+})
terms in (
3.8
). All together, this means that in
\mathcal{T}_{(t)}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{p})
, all terms involving quads
z_{k}\neq z_{j}
cancel out.

Step 2: Identifying the contributions to
\mathcal{T}_{(t)}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{p)}
around
z_{j}
:

Given the cancelations identified in
Step 1
, one can analyse the remaining contributions to
\mathcal{T}_{(t)}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{p)}
coming from corners belonging to the quad
z_{j}
. This reads as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{(t)}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{p})

\displaystyle=+\frac{m_{j}}{2}\Big[\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c^{+}_{j})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{j}}\rangle_{S(t)}-\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a^{+}_{j})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c^{+}_{j}}\rangle_{S(t)}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{m_{j}}{2}\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\Big[\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c^{+}_{j})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}-\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a^{+}_{j})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\chi_{c_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{m_{j}}{2}\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\Big[\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c^{+}_{j})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}-\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a^{+}_{j})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}\chi_{c_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad-m_{j}\Big[\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})-\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{-})\Big],

where the first line comes from
\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p})
, the second line comes from the term
\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})
in (
3.13
), the third line comes from the term
\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{-})
in (
3.13
) and the last line comes from the first two terms of (
3.13
).

Step 3: Computing
\mathcal{T}_{(t)}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{p)}
:

We are now ready to compute
\mathcal{T}_{(t)}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{p)}
. Recall the identifications of branching structures made in Section
2.5
summarised in Figure
2
. One can now use equations
\textrm{A}^{\prime}_{j},\textrm{B}^{\prime}_{j},\textrm{C}^{\prime}_{j},\textrm{D}^{\prime}_{j}
and
\textrm{A}^{\prime\prime}_{j},\textrm{B}^{\prime\prime}_{j},\textrm{C}^{\prime\prime}_{j},\textrm{D}^{\prime\prime}_{j}
similar to the ones introduced for the quad
z_{0}
in Section
2.5
. There is a natural dichotomy depending on the position SW,SE,NW or NE of the corner
\mathfrak{p}
around the quad
z_{j}
and the two following cases appear.

•

Case 1
: The corner
\mathfrak{p}
is belongs to SE or NW. We detail here the case where
\mathfrak{p}=a_{j}^{+}
, the other cases can be treated similarly. In that case one has
\mathfrak{p}^{+}=d_{j}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{p}^{-}=b_{j}^{-}
. We break the contributions of
\mathcal{T}_{(t)}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{p})
into several pieces regrouping the
\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a^{+}_{j})
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c_{j}^{+})
factors. In particular one has

\frac{m_{j}}{2}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a^{+}_{j})\Bigg[-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c^{+}_{j}}\rangle_{S(t)}+\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\chi_{c^{+}_{j}}\rangle_{S(t)}\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})+\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}\chi_{c^{+}_{j}}\rangle_{S(t)}\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\Bigg]=0,

(3.15)

\frac{m_{j}}{2}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c_{j}^{+})\Bigg[\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{j}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\chi_{a_{j}}\rangle_{S(t)}\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}\chi_{c_{j}}\rangle_{S(t)}\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\Bigg]\\
=m_{j}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c^{+}_{j}).

(3.16)

where (
3.15
) comes from
(\textrm{A}_{j}^{\prime\prime})
and (
3.16
) comes from
(\textrm{A}_{j}^{\prime})
.
Recalling that
\mathfrak{p}=a_{j}^{+}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}=d_{j}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{p}^{-}=b_{j}^{-}
, one has (adding the last line of the first equation of Step 2)

\mathcal{T}_{(t)}^{\prime}(a_{j}^{+})=m_{j}\Bigg[\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c^{+}_{j})+\cos(\theta_{j}^{(t)})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(b^{-}_{j})-\sin(\theta_{j}^{(t)})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(d^{+}_{j})\Bigg]=-m_{j}\mathcal{T}_{(t)}^{\prime}(c_{j}^{+}).

(3.17)

•

Case 2
: The corner
\mathfrak{p}
is belongs to SW or NE. We detail here the case where
\mathfrak{p}=b_{j}^{+}
, the other cases can be treated similarly. In that case one has
\mathfrak{p}^{+}=c_{j}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{p}^{-}=a_{j}^{-}
. We break once again the contributions of
\mathcal{T}_{(t)}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{p})
into several pieces regrouping the
\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a^{+}_{j})
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c_{j}^{+})
factors. In particular one has

\frac{m_{j}}{2}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a^{+}_{j})\Bigg[-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c^{+}_{j}}\rangle_{S(t)}+\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\chi_{c^{+}_{j}}\rangle_{S(t)}\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})+\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}\chi_{c^{+}_{j}}\rangle_{S(t)}\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\Bigg]\\
=-m_{j}\cos(\theta_{j}^{(t)})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a^{+}_{j}),

(3.18)

\frac{m_{j}}{2}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c_{j}^{+})\Bigg[\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{j}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{+}}\chi_{a_{j}}\rangle_{S(t)}\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{j})-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}^{-}}\chi_{c_{j}}\rangle_{S(t)}\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{j})\Bigg]\\
=m_{j}\sin(\theta_{j}^{(t)})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c^{+}_{j}),

(3.19)

where (
3.18
) comes from
(\textrm{B}_{j}^{\prime\prime})
and (
3.19
) comes from
(\textrm{B}_{j}^{\prime})
. Recalling that
\mathfrak{p}=b_{j}^{+}
,
\mathfrak{p}^{+}=c_{j}^{+}
and
\mathfrak{p}^{-}=a_{j}^{-}
allows to conclude that

\mathcal{T}_{(t)}^{\prime}(b_{j}^{+})=m_{j}\Bigg[+\cos(\theta_{j}^{(t)})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a^{+}_{j})+\sin(\theta_{j}^{(t)})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c^{+}_{j})\\
-\sin(\theta_{j}^{(t)})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{+})+\cos(\theta_{j}^{(t)})\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}^{-})\Bigg]=0.

(3.20)

Step 4: Concluding the proof

The previous construction ensures that
(\mathcal{T}_{(t)})^{\prime}=B\times\mathcal{T}_{(t)}
for some fixed matrix
B
and
\mathcal{T}_{(0)}=0
(as
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
is an
s
-embedding of
S(0)
). Therefore, one can apply the Cauchy-Lipchitz theorem allows to conclude the proof.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

To lighten the notations and the proof, we stated the previous Theorem assuming that all the masses
(m_{e})_{e\in\Lambda}
are constant along the deformation process. In full generality, assuming that

\theta^{(t)}_{e}:=\theta^{(0)}_{e}+m_{e}(t),

(3.21)

for some collection of
\mathcal{C}^{1}
smooth functions
t\mapsto m_{e}(t)
, then Lemma
3.2
still holds, with a differential equation becoming

\frac{d\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p})}{dt}=\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{z_{k}\in\Lambda}m_{k}^{\prime}(t)\Bigg[\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}-\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}\Bigg].

(3.22)

The previous proof applies almost verbatim, the only minor change is that
(\mathcal{T}_{(t)})^{\prime}=B(t)\times\mathcal{T}_{(t)}
for some time dependent matrix
B(t)
where the coefficients
m_{j}
are now replaced by
m_{j}^{\prime}(t)
. As the Cauchy-Lipchitz theorem is still valid in that setup this allows to conclude exactly in the same way.

Remark 3.4
.

The deformation procedure presented in Lemma
3.2
provides very basic discrete-level explanations regarding the lack of holomorphicity of the massive models as highlighted by the works of Park
[
75
,
74
]
. At time
t=0
, one can see using (
2.16
)

\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(c^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(0)}-\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(a^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(0)}=r_{z_{k}}\overline{F_{\mathfrak{p}}(z_{k})}\big(\cos(\theta_{k})\sin(\theta_{k})\big)^{-1},

(3.23)

where
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
is the
s
-holomorphic complexification of
\mathfrak{q}\mapsto\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle
. The function
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
is
discrete holomorphic
in the usual sense on the square lattice corresponding to the
s
-embedding
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
. In the uniform mass case, this observation yields that the deformation process using differential equations inserts to the conformal structure attached to the massive Ising model some
anti-holomorphic
component. This hints why the conformal structure of massive models is adapted to fermions satisfying some more evolved equation than
\partial_{z}f=0
in the usual Euclidian metric.

4.
The embedding of the massive-Ising model via continuous deformation

In this section we study in greater details the effective output of the embedding deformation induced by (
3.8
) when passing from the critical square lattice to the so called massive regime. Beyond the results themselves, we see this section as simplified introduction to the method we will use in Section
5
to treat the near-critical i.i.d. model. One works in the box
\Lambda_{n}
, centred at the origin. Let us start at time
t=0
from the Ising model on the critical square lattice, with uniform critical weights
\theta_{k}=\frac{\pi}{4}
for all edges
e_{k}\in\Lambda_{n}
. Let
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
be its standard embedding onto
\mathbb{C}
, made a tilling of squares of mesh size
\frac{\sqrt{2}}{n}
. One can see the bipartite graph
\mathcal{S}(\Lambda(\mathbb{Z}^{2}))=\mathcal{S}(G^{\bullet})\cup\mathcal{S}(G^{\circ})
as an isoradial grid, where all edges have length
\frac{1}{n}
. Our goal is to move continuously each Ising weight
\theta_{k}=2\arctan(x_{e_{k}})
from
\frac{\pi}{4}
to
\frac{\pi}{4}+\frac{m_{k}}{n}
while capturing how the family of embeddings
(\mathcal{S}^{(t)})_{t\leq\frac{1}{n}}
deviates from
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
.
In order to control this deviation along the deformation process, two main difficulties arise. The first one is to check that the embedding
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}
is proper, which allows use the two-point fermion estimate (
2.23
). The other point that requires some careful attention is to ensure that
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}
didn’t deviate already too much from
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
, which allows to keep applying (
2.23
) with the same pre-factor
\Theta
attached to the some fixed in advance
r_{0},\theta_{0}
parameters for
Unif(
\delta
)
grids. Indeed, this pre-factor comes from the regularity theory of
s
-holomorphic functions presented Section
2.4
and could in principle degenerate along the deformation process. Once this is done, controlling the deviation of
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}
from
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
becomes some standard Lotka–Volterra predator–prey problem that can be smoothly treated. The output of the construction is that the massive deformation via (
3.8
) with initial condition
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
produces after a time
t=\frac{1}{n}
a proper s-embedding that satisfies
Unif(
\delta,r_{0},\theta_{0}
)
with
\delta=\frac{1}{n}
and well chosen constants
r_{0},\theta_{0}
, at least if all the masses are bounded by a small enough
m
. We focus on the effects of the deformation (
3.8
) on the corners
\mathfrak{p}
such that
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(\mathfrak{p})
belongs to a square
\mathcal{D}_{2}
of width 2 centred at the origin. One naturally identifies the isoradial grid of mesh size
\frac{1}{n}
in
\mathcal{D}_{2}
with
\Lambda_{2n}
. There are
O(n^{2})
different corners inside
\mathcal{D}_{2}
. For two neighbouring corners
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}
inside
\Lambda_{n}
belonging to the quad
z_{j}
, set (as long as
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}
is proper)

M^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p}):=|\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p})-\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(\mathfrak{p})|,

(4.1)

\phi^{(t)}_{z_{j}}(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}):=\arg\phi_{v(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}),z_{j}}^{(t)},

(4.2)

where
\phi_{v(\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}),z_{j}}^{(t)}
is the
geometrical
half-angle at
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}(v_{\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}})
along the bisector linking
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}(v_{\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{p}^{\prime}})
to
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}(z_{j})
.
Finally, set

M(t):=\max\limits_{\mathfrak{p}\in\Lambda_{2n}}M^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p})

(4.3)

In what follows, the constant
\Theta
corresponds to the one in (
2.23
) for
s
-embeddings satisfying
Unif(
\delta,r_{0},\theta_{0}
)
with
r_{0}=10
and
\theta_{0}=\frac{\pi}{10}
. We are now ready to prove the next proposition, which quantifies the deviation from of
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}
to
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
for times
0\leq t\leq\frac{1}{n}
, as long as
m
is chosen small enough. This will be done using standard deviation estimates for ordinary differential equations which allows to keep track of the control of the rate of growth of the full-plane fermion (
2.23
) along the deformation. We will always verify the effect of the deformation of the coupling constants inside
\Lambda_{n}
on the
s
-embedding
\mathcal{S}(\Lambda_{2n})
in order to apply the properness principle recalled in Section
2.7
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Assume that the masses
(m_{e})_{e\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
vanish outside
\Lambda_{n}
and are bounded by (some small) enough
m>0
inside
\Lambda_{n}
. Then for any
0\leq t\leq\frac{1}{n}
, any corner
\mathfrak{p}\in\Lambda_{2n}
, any
v_{1}\neq v_{2}\in\Lambda_{2n}
one has:

(i)

The deviation of
\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}
can be bounded via

|\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p})-\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(\mathfrak{p})|\leq\frac{0.1}{n^{\frac{1}{2}}}\times nt

(4.4)

(ii)

The embedding
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}(\Lambda_{2n})
is proper, and distances in
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}
satisfy

0.9\leq\frac{|\mathcal{S}^{(t)}(v_{1})-\mathcal{S}^{(t)}(v_{2})|}{|\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(v_{1})-\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(v_{2})|}\leq 1.1

(4.5)

(iii)

The embedding
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}(\Lambda_{2n})
satisfies
Unif(
\frac{1}{n},10,\frac{\pi}{10}
)
.

Before diving into the details of the proof, let us highlight the strategy we repeatedly use. One first trivially bounds the two-points fermions by
1
, and derives that
\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}
didn’t deviate too much from
\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}
, at least for a small enough time
O(n^{-2})
. The control of the deviation of
\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}
implies that
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}(\Lambda_{2n})
is still proper and still satisfies
Unif(
\frac{1}{n},10,\frac{\pi}{10}
)
. Therefore, one can now use the improved bounds (
2.23
) and obtain, using the Grownwall lemma, a sharp (up to constant) order of magnitude of the effective deviation between
\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}
during that
O(n^{-2})
time span. Applying recursively this procedure
n
times over intervals of lenght
O(n^{-2})
allows to conclude.

Proof.

The proof will be made by recursion over indices
0\leq\ell\leq n-1
, showing that
(i),(ii),(iii)
hold for any time
t\in[\frac{\ell}{n^{2}};\frac{\ell+1}{n^{2}}]
.

Initialisation with

\ell=0
. We prove here that
(i),(ii),(iii)
hold
0\leq t\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}
. The proof splits in several elementary that we highlight here.

Step 1: Evaluating the deviation of
\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}
using trivial bounds on
\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle

Denote first

\varphi_{n}(s):=\max\limits_{\mathfrak{p}\in\Lambda_{2n}}\sum\limits_{z_{k}\in\Lambda_{n}}|\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(s)}|+|\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(s)}|.

(4.6)

There are at most
O(n^{2})
Kadanoff-Ceva fermions
\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle_{S(s)}
involved each sum used to define (
4.6
). Each Kadanoff-Ceva fermion is trivially bounded by
1
, independently of the time
s
, which ensures that
\varphi_{n}(s)=O(n^{2})
, for some absolute constant
O
, independent from
n
. For any corner
\mathfrak{p}\in\Lambda_{2n}
, one can integrate the ODE (
3.8
), which gives

\displaystyle|\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p})-\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(\mathfrak{p})|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int\limits_{0}^{t}\sum\limits_{z_{k}\in\Lambda_{n}}m_{k}\big(\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}(c^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(s)}-\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}(a^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}}\rangle_{S(s)}\big)ds\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int\limits_{0}^{t}m\sum\limits_{z_{k}\in\Lambda_{n}}\big|\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(c^{+}_{k})\big|\cdot\big|\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(s)}\big|+\big|\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(c^{+}_{k})\big|\cdot\big|\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(s)}\big|

\displaystyle\quad+\int\limits_{0}^{t}m\sum\limits_{z_{k}\in\Lambda_{n}}\big|\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}(c^{+}_{k})-\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(c^{+}_{k})\big|\cdot\big|\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(s)}\big|

\displaystyle\quad+\int\limits_{0}^{t}m\sum\limits_{z_{k}\in\Lambda_{n}}\big|\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}(a^{+}_{k})-\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(a^{+}_{k})\big|\cdot\big|\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(s)}\big|,

where in the above inequality we used that

•

For
e_{k}\in\Lambda_{n}
, its mass is bounded by
m
i.e.
\big|m_{k}\big|\leq m
.

•

The triangular inequality allows to decompose for
0\leq s\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}(a^{+}_{k})\big|

\displaystyle=\big|\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}(a^{+}_{k})-\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(a^{+}_{k})+\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(a^{+}_{k})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\big|\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(a^{+}_{k})\big|+\big|\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}(a^{+}_{k})-\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(a^{+}_{k})\big|.

For the standard square embedding
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
of the critical model, one has
\big|\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(a^{+}_{k})\big|=\big|\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(c^{+}_{k})\big|=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Therefore, taking the maximum of the above equality over all corners
\mathfrak{p}\in\Lambda_{2n}
ensures that

M(t)\leq\frac{m\cdot K(t)}{n^{\frac{1}{2}}}+m\int\limits_{0}^{t}M(s)\varphi_{n}(s)ds,

(4.7)

with an increasing non-negative function
K(t):=\int\limits_{0}^{t}\varphi_{n}(s)ds
. One can now apply the standard Grownwall lemma to (
4.7
) which ensures that

M(t)\leq m\cdot\frac{K(t)}{n^{\frac{1}{2}}}\times\exp(m\cdot K(t)).

(4.8)

Since
\varphi_{n}(s)=O(n^{2})
, the function
K
is uniformly bounded in
[0;\frac{1}{n^{2}}]
, uniformly in
n
. Taking
m
small enough ensures that for any
s\in[0;\frac{1}{n^{2}}]
and
\mathfrak{p}\in\Lambda_{2n}
, one has

|\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}(\mathfrak{p})-\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(\mathfrak{p})|\leq M(s)\leq\frac{0.0001}{n^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

(4.9)

Step 2: Verifying that
\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(\Lambda_{2n})
is proper and comparable to
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
The chore of this second step is to prove the distance comparability
(ii)
, which allows to deduce both properness of
\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(\Lambda_{2n})
and that it satisfies
Unif(
\frac{1}{n},10,\frac{\pi}{10}
)
. For any pair of distinct vertices
v,v^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{G}
, there exist an injective sequence
(\mathfrak{q}_{j})_{j\leq d_{G}(v,v^{\prime})}
sequence of
d_{G}(v,v^{\prime})\leq 4n\cdot|\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(v)-\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(v^{\prime})|
neighbouring corners such that

\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(v)-\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(v^{\prime})=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d_{G}(v,v^{\prime})}(-1)^{j}\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}(\mathfrak{q}_{j})^{2}.

Provided
m
is chosen small enough (as in Step 1), (
4.9
) implies that for
0\leq s\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}

\bigg|\big(\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}(\mathfrak{q}_{j})\big)^{2}-\big(\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(\mathfrak{q}_{j})\big)^{2}\bigg|\leq\frac{0.0025}{n}.

(4.10)

Summing brutally the errors along the path
(\mathfrak{q}_{j})_{j\leq d_{G}(v,v^{\prime})}
ensures that

|\frac{\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(v)-\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(v^{\prime})}{\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(v)-\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(v^{\prime})}-1|\leq\sum\limits_{j=1}^{d_{G}(v,v^{\prime})}\frac{0.0025}{n}\leq 0.01,

(4.11)

concluding that the distance comparability in
(ii)
holds. This also ensures
that
\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(\Lambda_{2n})
is proper. To verify this statement, one first notes that (
4.10
) ensures that for any quad
z\in\diamondsuit(G)
, the orientations of the tangential quadrilaterals
\mathcal{S}^{(0),\diamond}(z)
and
\mathcal{S}^{(s),\diamond}(z)
are the same, as the fermion
\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}
didn’t move enough from its original value to modify the orientation of the image of a tangential quad. Moreover, one use a specific choice of vertices
v,v^{\prime}
in (
4.11
). More precisely, fix first
\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(v=0)=0_{\mathbb{C}}
(i.e. one should fix a reference point for the embedding and we declare here the image of the origin of
\Lambda_{2n}
to be the origin of the complex plane). One can then fix
v^{\prime}\in\gamma_{\Lambda_{2n}}
, the (oriented in the positive direction) boundary of the
combinatorial
box
\Lambda_{2n}
(defined in the context of Section
2.7
). It is clear from (
4.11
) that

0.99\leq\frac{|\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(v^{\prime})-\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(0)|}{|\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(v^{\prime})-\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(0)|}\leq 1.01,

which ensures that
\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(\gamma_{\Lambda_{2n}})
only winds once around the origin of the plane. Moreover, it is a simple curve, as any self-intersection would make (
4.11
) would fail. Therefore, one can apply once again the argument principle reasoning presented in Section
2.7
to conclude that
\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(\Lambda_{2n})
is indeed proper. To conclude on the item
(iii)
, it is enough to recall that the original square lattice
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
satisfies
Unif(
\frac{1}{n},10,\frac{\pi}{10}
)
with a pretty fair margin (all edge-lengths are
\frac{1}{n}
and all angles are
\frac{\pi}{2}
in
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
) to see that the deviation (
4.9
) implies that
\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(\Lambda_{2n})
still satisfies
Unif(
\frac{1}{n},10,\frac{\pi}{10}
)
.

Step 3: Using the improved bounds (
2.23
)
Once we know that all embeddings
\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(\Lambda_{2n})
are proper and satisfy
Unif(
\frac{1}{n},10,\frac{\pi}{10}
)
, it is possible, when working with the fermion
\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}
, to replace the trivial bounds of the form
|\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle|\leq 1
by the accurate one (
2.23
). Applying (
4.9
) and the distance comparability of the item
(ii)
proven in Step 1 ensures that

|\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S(s)}|\leq\Theta\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}(a^{+}_{k})\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}(\mathfrak{p})|}{|\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(\mathfrak{p})-\mathcal{S}^{(s)}(a^{+}_{k})|}\leq\Theta\cdot\frac{(1.1)^{2}}{n}\frac{1}{0.9\cdot|\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(\mathfrak{p})-\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(a_{k})|}.

(4.12)

Taking the maximum over corners
\mathfrak{p}\in\Lambda_{2n}
, one has

\displaystyle\varphi_{n}(s)

\displaystyle=\max_{\mathfrak{p}\in\Lambda_{n}}O\Bigg(\Theta\sum\limits_{z_{k}\in\Lambda_{n}}\frac{1}{n}\frac{1}{\cdot|\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(\mathfrak{p})-\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(z_{k})|}\Bigg)

(4.13)

\displaystyle=n\times\max_{\mathfrak{p}\in\Lambda_{n}}O\Bigg(\Theta\sum\limits_{z_{k}\in\Lambda_{n}}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\frac{1}{\cdot|\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(\mathfrak{p})-\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(z_{k})|}\Bigg)

(4.14)

\displaystyle=n\times O\Bigg(\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{D}_{1}}\frac{1}{|z-\mathfrak{p}|}dA(z)\Bigg)

(4.15)

\displaystyle=O(n),

(4.16)

where
O
independent from
m
and
n
and we passe from the second to the third line using the approximation of the discrete sum by its continuous area integral. Once can now redo verbatim the reasoning of Step
1
using this the improved bound
\varphi_{n}(s)=O(n)
in the Grownwall lemma (
4.8
). Hence, provided
m
chosen small enough, one has
M(s)\leq(0.01\cdot n^{-\frac{1}{2}})\times ns
, which concludes the proof of the initialisation.

Heredity
Assume that the proposition has been proven for all
0\leq\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell
. Let us prove that
(i),(ii),(iii)
hold for
\frac{\ell}{n^{2}}\leq t\leq\frac{\ell+1}{n^{2}}
. The recurrence hypothesis ensures that

\Big|\mathcal{Y}^{(\frac{\ell}{n^{2}})}(\mathfrak{p})-\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(\mathfrak{p})\Big|\leq\frac{0.1}{n^{\frac{1}{2}}}\cdot n\cdot\frac{\ell}{n^{2}}\leq\frac{0.1}{n^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Therefore, the embedding
\mathcal{S}^{(\frac{\ell}{n^{2}})}(\Lambda_{2n})
satisfies
Unif(
\frac{1}{n},10,\frac{\pi}{10}
)
with a fair margin. Therefore, one can redo almost verbatim the proof of the initialisation, running the same ODE for a time
0\leq s\leq n^{-2}
, but this time with a initial condition being the fermion
\mathcal{Y}^{(\frac{\ell}{n^{2}})}
. Since the deterministic constants on the line-to-line passage (e.g. the deterministic coefficients
0.0025
,
0.01
…) were chosen to be far from sharp, it is clear that the initial condition
|\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(a_{k}^{+})|=|\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(c_{k}^{+})|=n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
can be replaced by
0.9\cdot n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\leq|\mathcal{Y}^{(\frac{\ell}{n^{2}})}(a_{k}^{+})|,|\mathcal{Y}^{(\frac{\ell}{n^{2}})}(c_{k}^{+})|\leq 1.1\cdot n^{-\frac{1}{2}}
with no incidence in the proof nor the choice of the small enough mass
m
. This allows to deduce that one can keep the same
m>0
is in the initialisation step while still having for any
\frac{\ell}{n^{2}}\leq s\leq\frac{\ell+1}{n^{2}}
and any
\mathfrak{p}\in\Lambda_{2n}
the control

\Big|\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}(\mathfrak{p})-\mathcal{Y}^{(\frac{\ell}{n^{2}})}(\mathfrak{p})\Big|\leq\frac{0.1}{n^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Big(s-\frac{\ell}{n^{2}}\Big)\cdot n,

which allows to conclude that

\Big|\mathcal{Y}^{(s)}(\mathfrak{p})-\mathcal{Y}^{(0)}(\mathfrak{p})\Big|\leq\frac{0.1}{n^{\frac{1}{2}}}\cdot ns.

∎

Once Proposition
4.1
ensures that existence of an
s
-embedding of the massive square lattice (for a small enough mass), one can use the technology recalled in Theorem
1.4
together with standard monotonicity properties of the FK-Ising model to provide a new proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Consider the massive FK-Ising model on the annulus
\textrm{A}_{n}:=\Lambda_{n}\backslash\Lambda_{\frac{n}{2}}
