The key innovation of
[
15
]
is the understanding that constructing a good approximation of the standard continuous Laplacian on an
s
-embedding is enough to prove that discrete fermions converge to their natural continuous counterparts. When the lattice is the critical square lattice, the naive discretisation of the continuous Laplacian by the discrete one turns out to be sufficient.
Since our near-critical weakly random model is defined on an s-embedding that is
almost
a critical square lattice, we work with the so-called
s
-Laplacian, adapted to the embedding
\mathcal{S}
while also approximating well the standard discrete Laplacian on
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
. We keep exactly the notations
[
15
, Sections 3 and 4]
. This imples that

•

There exist an operator
\Delta_{S}
defined in
[
15
, Lemma 3.7]
for a function
H
one
\mathcal{S}(\Lambda(G))
, given for
v^{\bullet}\in G^{\bullet}
and
v^{\circ}\in G^{\circ}
by

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{S}}[H](v^{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{v_{k}^{\bullet}\sim v^{\bullet}}a_{v^{\bullet}v_{k}^{\bullet}}\big(H(v_{k}^{\bullet})-H(v^{\bullet})\big)+\sum\limits_{v_{k}^{\circ}\sim v^{\bullet}}b_{v^{\bullet}v_{k}^{\circ}}\big(H(v_{k}^{\circ})-H(v^{\bullet})\big),

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{S}}[H](v^{\circ})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{v_{k}^{\circ}\sim v^{\bullet}}b_{v^{\circ}v_{k}^{\bullet}}\big(H(v_{k}^{\bullet})-H(v^{\circ})\big)-\sum\limits_{v_{k}^{\circ}\sim v^{\circ}}a_{v^{\circ}v_{k}^{\circ}}\big(H(v_{k}^{\circ})-H(v_{k}^{\circ})\big).

where the coefficients
a_{vv^{\prime}}
are symmetric and positive, while the coefficients
b_{vv^{\prime}}
are also symmetric and real. In particular one has
a_{v^{\bullet}_{0}v_{1}^{\bullet}}=r_{z}^{-1}\sin^{2}(\theta_{z})
and
a_{v^{\circ}_{0}v_{1}^{\circ}}=r_{z}^{-1}\cos^{2}(\theta_{z})
for que quad
z=(v_{0}^{\bullet}v_{0}^{\circ}v_{1}^{\bullet}v_{1}^{\circ})
.

•

Some special attention is given to
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}^{(0)}}

, since it corresponds on the isoradial embedding of mesh size
\delta
(up to some explicit
\delta^{-1}
and sign factors) to usual Laplacian
\Delta^{\textrm{sq}}
for the standard random walk.
Following
[
26
]
), set for
v\in\Lambda^{\delta}(G)

\Delta^{\textrm{sq}}[H](v):=\frac{1}{4\delta^{2}}\sum\limits_{v_{k}\sim v}\Big(H(v_{k})-H(v)\Big).

(6.3)

When
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
is seen as an isoradial lattice of mesh size
\delta
, the coefficients
b_{vv^{\prime}}
vanish, while

\Delta_{\mathcal{S}^{(0)}}[H](v)=\pm_{v}\delta\Delta^{\textrm{sq}}[H](v),

(6.4)

where
\pm_{v}:=+
if
v\in G^{\bullet}
and
\pm_{v}:=-
if
v\in G^{\circ}
.

In particular, we are going to use the fact (proved in
[
26
]
) that
\Delta^{\textrm{sq}}
provides a very accurate approximation of the standard continuous Laplacian
\Delta=\partial_{xx}+\partial_{yy}
to deduce that
\Delta_{S}
also provides an very accurate (up to the sign) approximation of
\Delta
on the associated
s
-embedding.
We show that the naturally defined discrete differential operators on the
s
-embeddings
(\mathcal{S}^{(t)})_{0\leq t\leq t_{n}^{(\alpha)}}
approximate very well those on the square lattice, which themselves approximate very well the continuous ones. Let us state that, with a slightly more refined analysis, one could in principle take
\alpha=\alpha(n)
going to
0
slowly enough, creating some logarithmic corrections in the analysis. Still, deforming up to
any
power below
-\frac{2}{3}
allows to lighten the writing of the proofs.

Proposition 6.2
.

Consider an s-embedding
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}(\Lambda_{n})
constructed by Lemma
5.1
and a real function
\phi
. Then, with
\mathbf{P}
-probability at least
1-O(n^{-4})
, there exist a constant
O=O(\omega,\alpha)>0
such that for any
0\leq t\leq t^{(\alpha)}_{n}
,

[\Delta_{\mathcal{S}^{(t)}}\phi](v)=\pm_{v}\delta\big[\Delta\phi+O(\delta^{1+\alpha}\sup|D^{(2)}\phi|)+O(\delta^{2+\alpha}\sup|D^{(3)}\phi|)\big],

(6.5)

where the supremum are taken in a disc of size
10\delta
around
v
.

Proof.

Let us start with the intuition behind this statement. For
v\in G^{\bullet}
, one has

\Delta^{\textrm{sq}}[H](v)=\Delta\phi+O(\delta^{2}\sup|D^{(4)}\phi|).

(6.6)

This comes from the fact that, on the standard square lattice, when expanding
\phi(v_{k})
via the Taylor expansion around of
\phi
around
z
, the contribution of the terms of the form
\big(\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(v_{k})-\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(v)\big)^{\ell=0,1,2,3}
(together with associated conjugate and absolute values) exactly cancel. This can be seen in
[
26
, Lemma 2.2]
, whose result is even better when working exactly on the square lattice rather than simply an isoradial graph. Let us discuss how to obtain
O(\delta^{1+\alpha}\sup|D^{(2)}\phi|)
for the almost square lattice
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}
. Recall that it was proven in
[
15
, Proposition 3.12 ]
that
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}^{(t)}}[1]=\Delta_{\mathcal{S}^{(t)}}[\mathcal{S}^{(t)}]=\Delta_{\mathcal{S}^{(t)}}[\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{(t)}]=0
. Moreover, one can write for
v_{k}\sim v
and a real function
\phi

\displaystyle\phi(v_{k})

\displaystyle=\phi(v)+(\mathcal{S}^{(t)}(v_{k})-\mathcal{S}^{(t)}(v))\partial\phi(v)+(\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{(t)}(v_{k})-\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{(t)}(v))\overline{\partial}\phi(v)

\displaystyle+C_{\phi}(\mathcal{S}^{(t)}(v_{k})-\mathcal{S}^{(t)}(v))^{2}+C^{{}^{\prime}}_{\phi}(\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{(t)}(v_{k})-\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{(t)}(v))^{2}+C^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{\phi}|\mathcal{S}^{(t)}(v_{k})-\mathcal{S}^{(t)}(v)|^{2}

\displaystyle+O(\delta^{3}\sup|D^{(3)}\phi|),

where the real coefficients
C_{\phi},C^{{}^{\prime}}_{\phi}
and
C^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{\phi}
are bounded by
\sup|D^{(2)}\phi|
. As recalled in the proof of
[
15
, Proposition 3.12]

\mathcal{S}
only satisfies some
Unif(
\delta
)
one has
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}}[\mathcal{S}^{2}]=\Delta_{S}[\big(\mathcal{S}(\cdot)-\mathcal{S}(z)\big)^{2}]=O(\delta)
only using very crude bounds
O(\delta^{-1})
on the coefficients
a_{vv^{\prime}}
and
b_{vv^{\prime}}
. If one works with
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
, then
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}^{(0)}}[(\mathcal{S}^{(0)})^{2}]=0
by the discussion recalled above and the link to
\Delta^{\textrm{sq}}
. Using that the local geometry of
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}
is almost a square lattice up to an
1+o(\delta^{\alpha})
multiplicative factor everywhere, one can directly see the the improvement to the crude bound
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}^{ts)}}[(\mathcal{S}^{(t)})^{2}]=O(\delta)
is by an
O(\delta^{1+\alpha})
multiplicative factor. This can easily be carried out carefully using e.g. the factorisation
\Delta_{S}=-4\overline{\partial}_{\omega}\partial_{\mathcal{S}}
defined in
[
15
, Proposition 3.7]
(here
\omega
is kept for the sake of notations consistency with
[
15
]
and is not related to the
\mathbf{P}
-randomness). The last terms involving the third derivatives of
\phi
can be treated similarly, as the contribution
\big(\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(v_{k})-\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(v)\big)^{3}
cancel on the square lattice. The analysis is exactly the same for
v\in G^{\circ}
.
∎

We now prove that, already at discrete level, the function
H_{F^{(t)}_{\delta_{n},\omega}}
is (quantitatively) close to its continuous harmonic continuation, except maybe in a
\delta^{1-\eta}
layer close to the boundary of a domain, following closely a simplified version
[
15
, Theorem 4.1]
and
[
78
, Chapter 4]
. Denote by
\Omega^{\delta}_{\textrm{int}(\eta)}
the main connected component of the
\delta^{1-\eta}
interior of
\Omega^{\delta}
.

Theorem 6.3
.

Consider an s-embedding
\mathcal{S}^{(t)}_{\omega}=\mathcal{S}^{\delta_{n}}
constructed by Lemma
5.1
and
(\Omega^{\delta},a^{\delta},b^{\delta},\omega,t)\subset\mathbb{C}
a discrete bounded simply connected domain drawn over
\mathcal{S}^{\delta_{n}}
, equipped with Dobrushin boundary conditions. For a realisation
\omega
under the probability measure
\mathbf{P}
, set
F^{(t)}_{\delta_{n},\omega}
the
s
-holomorphic function associated to the FK-Dobrushin observable
X^{(t)}_{(\Omega^{\delta_{n}},\omega)}
. Denote by
h^{(t)}_{\mathrm{int}(\eta),\omega}
the harmonic continuation of
H_{F^{(t)}_{\delta_{n},\omega}}
in
\Omega^{\delta}_{\mathrm{int}(\eta)}
. Then, with
\mathbf{P}
-probability at least
1-O(n^{-4})
, there exist an exponent
\gamma=\gamma(\alpha)>0
and constant
O=O(\alpha,\omega)>0
such that for any
0\leq t\leq t^{(\alpha)}_{n}
and uniformly in
\Omega^{\delta}_{\mathrm{int}(\eta)}
, one has

|H_{F^{(t)}_{\delta_{n},\omega}}-h^{(t)}_{\mathrm{int}(\eta),\omega}|=O(\delta_{n}^{\gamma}).

(6.7)

We follow closely the proof of
[
15
]
the lighten the derivation and mostly highlight the differences with our proof. Fix a non-negative symmetric function
\phi_{0}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{C})
which vanishes outside of
\mathbb{D}(0,\frac{1}{2})
with
\int_{\mathbb{C}}\phi_{0}(u)dA(u)=1
. Let
0<\varepsilon\ll\eta
some small parameter to be later. Set

d_{u}:=\textrm{dist}(u,\partial\Omega^{\delta})\textrm{ and }\rho_{u}:=\delta^{\varepsilon}\textrm{crad}(u,\partial\Omega^{\delta})\asymp\delta^{\varepsilon}d_{u}\gg\delta\textrm{ for }u\in\Omega^{\delta}_{\textrm{int}(\eta)},

(6.8)

where
\textrm{crad}(u,\partial\Omega^{\delta})
is the conformal radius of
u
in the domain
\Omega^{\delta}
. The map
u\mapsto\textrm{crad}(u,\partial\Omega^{\delta})
is smooth its gradient is uniformly bounded and its second derivative is bounded by
O(d_{u}^{-1})
. This allows to define the running mollifier
\phi(w,u)
by setting

\phi(w,u):=\rho_{u}^{-2}\phi_{0}(\rho_{u}^{-1}(w-u))\textrm{ for }u\in\Omega^{\delta}_{\textrm{int}(\eta)},

(6.9)

and the mollified approximation of
H_{F^{(t)}_{\delta_{n},\omega}}=H_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}
by setting

\widetilde{H}_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(u):=\int\limits_{B(u,\rho_{u})}\phi(w,u)H_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(w)dA(w),

(6.10)

where
H_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}
is continued in a piecewise linear manner from
\Lambda(G)\cup\diamondsuit(G)
in
\Omega^{\delta}_{\textrm{int}(\eta)}
. Below, we will prove the following key estimate

|\Delta\widetilde{H}_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(u)|=O(\delta^{\gamma}d_{u}^{-2+\gamma}),

(6.11)

which can be seen as a optimisation (that we will keep true step after step) of the estimate

|\widetilde{H}_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(u)|=O(\delta^{p}d_{u}^{-2-q})+O(\delta^{1-s}d_{u}^{-3}),

(6.12)

where
p>q(1-\eta)
and
s<\eta
. Once this is done, one can conclude exactly as for
[
15
, Theorem 4.1]

Proof of the key estimate
(
6.11
).

We work here on the event where all the
s
-embeddings
(\mathcal{S}^{(t)})_{0\leq t\leq t_{n}^{(\alpha)}}
obtained via Lemma
5.1
satisfy
Unif(
\frac{1}{n},10,\frac{\pi}{10}
)
, the fermions encoding the embedding are close enough to the square lattice and satisfy the Lipchitzness of Lemma
6.1
.
As the mollifier
\phi(\cdot,u)
vanishes near the boundary of
B(u,\rho_{u})
one has for
u\in\Omega^{\delta}_{\textrm{int}(\eta)}

\Delta\widetilde{H}_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(u)=\int\limits_{B(u,\rho_{u})}\big(\Delta_{u}\phi(w,u)\big)H_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(w)dA(w).

(6.13)

Step 1: Repeat Step 1 in
[
15
, Section 4.2]
: Replace
\Delta_{u}\phi(w,u)
by
\Delta_{w}\phi(w,u)
. Since the
s
-holomorphic functions are Lipchitz and the mollifier is symmetric one has, exactly as in
[
15
, Section 4.2]

\Delta\widetilde{H}_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(u)=\int\limits_{B(u,\rho_{u})}\big(\Delta_{w}\phi(w,u)\big)H_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(w)dA(w)+O(\delta^{\varepsilon}\cdot\delta^{2\varepsilon}\cdot\rho_{u}^{-2}).

(6.14)

This keeps (
6.12
) true with
p=\varepsilon
and
q=0
.

Step 2: Replacing the integral by a discrete approximation

Consider for each
v\in\Omega^{\delta}_{\textrm{int}(\eta)}\cap\Lambda(G)
the quadrilateral
\square_{v}^{(t)}
, which is ’almost’ a square of width
\sqrt{2}^{-1}\delta
whose extremal vertices are the four quads
z_{1,2,3,4}
neighbouring
v
. For each
w\in\square_{v}
, one gets via simple computations that

\Delta_{w}\phi(w,u)\cdot H_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(w)=\pm_{v}\big[\Delta_{v}\phi(v,u)+O(\frac{\delta}{\rho_{u}^{5}})\big]\cdot\big[H_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(v)+O(\frac{\delta}{d_{u}})\big].

(6.15)

Thus one has

\displaystyle\int\limits_{B(u,\rho_{u})}\big(\Delta_{w}\phi(w,u)\big)H_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(w)dA(w)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{v\in B(u,\rho_{u})}\Delta_{v}\phi(v,u)H_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(v)\textrm{Area}(\square_{v}^{(t)})

\displaystyle\quad+O(\frac{\delta}{\rho_{u}^{3}})+O(\frac{\delta}{d_{u}\times\rho_{u}^{2}})+O(\frac{\delta^{2-3\varepsilon}}{d_{u}^{4}}),

where in the error
\frac{\delta}{\rho_{u}^{3}}
comes from integrating the error
\frac{\delta}{\rho_{u}^{5}}
over a ball of area
\rho_{u}^{2}
and bounding
|H_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}|
by
1
, the error
\frac{\delta}{d_{u}\times\rho_{u}^{2}}
comes from the trivial bound
|\Delta_{v}\phi(v,u)|=\rho_{u}^{-4}
integrated over a ball of area
\rho_{u}^{2}
and the last error is the integrated version of the product of the errors
\frac{\delta}{\rho_{u}^{5}}
and
\frac{\delta}{d_{u}}
. This keeps (
6.12
) true with
s=3\varepsilon
and
p>1-\eta
. Moreover, it is not hard to see that if
\varepsilon
is chosen small enough compared to
\alpha
, one can replace all the terms
\textrm{Area}(\square_{v}^{(t)})
by
\frac{1}{2}\delta^{2}
(using that
\textrm{Area}(\square_{v}^{(t)})=\frac{1}{2}\delta^{2}+O(\delta^{2+\alpha})
) while keeping the estimate (
6.12
) true with
s=3\varepsilon
and
p>1-\eta
.

Step 3: Replace
\Delta_{v}\phi(v,u)
by
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}^{(t)}}[\phi(\cdot,u)]
and integrate by parts

One can now use Proposition
6.2
writing

\Delta_{v}\phi(v,u)=\frac{\pm_{v}}{\delta}\Bigg[\Delta_{\mathcal{S}^{(t)}}[\phi_{u}](v)+O(\frac{\delta^{1+\alpha}}{\rho_{u}^{4}})+O(\frac{\delta^{2+\alpha}}{\rho_{u}^{5}})\Bigg].

(6.16)

Summing over the vertices
v\in B(u,\rho_{u})
allows to include the correction terms (provided
\varepsilon
is small enough with respect to
\alpha
), in the error
O
, compatible with (
6.12
) and allows to rewrite

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{v\in B(u,\rho_{u})}\delta^{2}\Delta_{v}\phi(v,u)H_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{v\in B(u,\rho_{u})}\delta\Delta_{\mathcal{S}^{(t)}}[\phi_{u}](v)\Big(\pm_{v}H_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(v)\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{v\in B(u,\rho_{u})}\delta\Delta_{\mathcal{S}^{(t)}}\Big[\pm_{v}H_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(v)\Big]\phi_{u}(v).

Step 4: Conclude

We are now in position to conclude. Recalling
[
15
, (4.14) in Remark 4.2]
, the fact that the
s
-holomorphic functions are Lipschitz ensures that

H_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(v)=O(\frac{\delta^{2}}{d_{u}^{3}}),

which ensures that

\sum\limits_{v\in B(u,\rho_{u})}\delta\Delta_{\mathcal{S}^{(t)}}\Big[\pm_{v}H_{F^{(t)}_{\omega}}(v)\Big]\phi_{u}(v)=O(\frac{\delta}{d_{u}^{3}}).

This concludes the proof. Note that only some
\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon
Holder exponent for
s
-holomorphic functions in Lemma
6.1
would have been enough to conclude.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

As already mentioned, one could have replace
\alpha
by
\alpha(n)
going to
0
at a logarithmic speed and still have obtained (modifying continuous statement
[
15
, Lemma A.2]
) some bound going to
0
as
\delta\to 0
replacing the polynomial bound
O(\delta^{\gamma})
(which depends on
\alpha
) in the statement of Theorem
6.3
. This would have allowed to conclude similarly for the convergence of SLE curves.

We are now in position to prove Theorem
1.6

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

This proof will be a simple gathering of all the facts already proven. To simplify the reading, fix some
\alpha>0
and write
t_{n}=t_{n}^{(\alpha)}
. Consider a realisation
\omega
obtained under
\mathbf{P}
. Then with probability at least
1-O(n^{-4})
, on the box
\Lambda_{n}
, one has

•

The
s
-embedding
\mathcal{S}_{\omega}^{(t_{n})}(\Lambda_{n})
is proper and satisfies
Unif(
\frac{1}{n},10,\frac{\pi}{10}
)
by Proposition
5.3
.

•

The FK-Ising model on
\mathcal{S}_{\omega}^{(t_{n})}(\Lambda_{n})
satisfies the strong box crossing at each scale by Theorem
1.2
.

•

There exist
O=O(\omega)
and
\gamma=\gamma(\omega,\alpha)>0
such that

|H_{F^{(t_{n})}_{\delta_{n},\omega}}-h^{(t_{n})}_{\mathrm{int}(\eta),\omega}|=O(n^{-\gamma}).

One can now apply the Borel-Cantelli lemma, which ensures that there exist a set
A^{(\alpha)}
of measure
1
for
\mathbf{P}
such that for any
\omega\in A^{(\alpha)}
, there exist
n_{0}(\omega)
such that the three properties listed above hold for any
n\geq n_{0}(\omega)
. Fix a simply connected domain
(\Omega,a,b)\subset[-\frac{1}{2};\frac{1}{2}]^{2}
with two marked boundary points
a,b\in\partial\Omega
, seen as prime-ends, approximated in the Carathéodory sense by
(\Omega_{\delta_{n}},a^{(\delta_{n})},b^{(\delta_{n})})\subset\mathcal{S}^{(t_{n})}
with the associated Dobrushin boundary conditions. For any
\omega\in A^{(\alpha)}

n\geq n_{0}
, the first two properties ensures that the family of curves
\big(\widetilde{\gamma}^{(\delta_{n})}_{\omega,t_{n}}\big)_{n\geq 1}

drawn on the embedding

\mathcal{S}^{(t_{n})}(\Lambda_{n})
is pre-compact. Moreover, one can reproduce verbatim the proof of
[
15
, Section 4.3]
, which proves that, for any
\omega\in A^{(\alpha)}
, the family of complexified FK-observables
(F^{(t_{n})}_{\delta_{n},\omega})_{n\geq 1}
converge to a function
f_{\Omega}
as
n\to\infty
, uniformly on compacts of
\Omega
. The function is given by
f_{\Omega}(z)=\sqrt{\Phi^{\prime}(z)}
, where
\Phi:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\times]0,1[
is the uniformisation of
\Omega
to the strip that sends
a,b
to respectively
\pm\infty
. It is a classical fact (see
[
51
,
53
,
52
]
) that the pre-compactness of the family of curves
\big(\widetilde{\gamma}^{(\delta_{n})}_{\omega,t_{n}}\big)_{n\geq 1}
already discussed together with the convergence of the associated FK observables ensure that the curves
\big(\widetilde{\gamma}^{(\delta_{n})}_{\omega,t_{n}}\big)_{n\geq 1}
converge to the chordal
\textrm{SLE}(16/3,\Omega,a,b)
. Moreover, for any
\omega\in A^{(\alpha)}
, when choosing a commun reference point to
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}(\Lambda_{n})\subset\delta_{n}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
\mathcal{S}^{(t_{n})}(\Lambda_{n})
, the distances are all multiplied by some
1+o_{n\to\infty}(1)
factor (where
o_{n\to\infty}(1)
depends on
\omega
). There, one can trivially couple the curves
\widetilde{\gamma}^{(\delta_{n})}_{\omega,t_{n}}\in\mathcal{S}^{(t_{n})}(\Lambda_{n})
and
\gamma^{(\delta_{n})}_{\omega,t_{n}}\in\delta_{n}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
to have exactly the same exploration process. Using this coupling between the curves, the curves
\big(\widetilde{\gamma}^{(\delta_{n})}_{\omega,t_{n}}\big)_{n\geq 1}
and
\gamma^{(\delta_{n})}_{\omega,t_{n}}\in\delta_{n}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
are drawn at an
o_{n\to\infty}(1)
distance from each other as
n\to\infty
. Recalling the topology for the convergence of curves
[
72
]
and using the convergence of the interfaces
\big(\widetilde{\gamma}^{(\delta_{n})}_{\omega,t_{n}}\big)_{n\geq 1}
allows to conclude for
\big(\gamma^{(\delta_{n})}_{\omega,t_{n}}\big)_{n\geq 1}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

Once we know that with high probability
\mathcal{S}^{(t_{n}^{(\alpha)}}
and
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
are very close to each other, one can apply verbatim
[
62
]
. Note that an easy computation coming from the study of the stochastic derivatives (see e.g. Step
0
of the proof of Proposition
5.3
) that for
\mathbb{E}_{S(t_{n}^{(\alpha)})}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]=\mathbb{E}_{S(0)}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]+\asymp\sqrt{t_{n}^{(\alpha)}}
, where
\asymp
is taken up to logarithmic correction. In particular the deviation of is much larger than the
n
scaling factor coming from the fermion when
\alpha
is small enough.
∎

Proof of the second part of Theorem
1.8
.

We do not provide a proof here, only a sketch. Assume that
t_{n}:=\log(n)^{-\beta}
. As mentioned in Remark
6.4
, we claim that if
\beta
is chosen large enough, one can redo the proof of the Laplacian estimate of Proposition
6.2
and Theorem
6.3
, with an exponent
\alpha=\alpha(n)\to 0
producing a correction decaying logarithmically fast to
0
(with a large enough exponent). Modifying the proof of
[
15
, Lemma A.2]
), one can replace the RHS
O(\delta^{\gamma}_{n})
in the statement of Theorem
6.3
by some quantity that goes to
0
as
\delta_{n}\to 0
. This allows to apply verbatim the proof of chordal SLE converges presented above. Finally, the convergence of the energy density is once again a direct application of
[
62
]
. ∎

Appendix A
Appendix

We first briefly discuss how to prove Lemma
6.1
, which states that one provided that the
s
-embeddings are obtained from the SDE of Lemma
5.1
, as long as all coordinates of the embedding
\mathcal{S}^{(s)}
are close to those of
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
, then one can keep the Lipchitzness of
s
-holomorphic functions the embedding
\mathcal{S}^{(s)}
similar to the one on
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
which corresponds to the critical square lattice where the result is known.

Sketch of the proof of Lemma
6.1
.

Let
F^{(s)}
an
s
-holomorphic function on
\mathcal{S}^{(z)}
. Recall from
[
15
, Section 2.5]
that
\operatorname{Re}[F^{(s)}]
is
harmonic
for the backward random walk on the
s
-graph
\mathcal{S}^{(s)}-i\mathcal{Q}^{(s)}
, for the weights recalled in
[
15
, Proposition 2.16 and (2.20)]
. At time
s=0
, this random walk can be seen as the standard random walk on vertices of the square lattice with
\frac{1}{4}
transitions to the neighbours (see e.g.
[
18
, Section 3.2.2]
). The underlying Laplacian is denoted by
\Delta^{(0)}
, with no additional
\delta^{2}
scaling compared to the one used Proposition
6.2
.
We claim that first that provided
\mathcal{S}^{(s)}
remains close enough to
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
, this random remains can still be identified random walk on the square lattice, with weights (depending on the local geometry) that are almost
\frac{1}{4}
everywhere. We follow the formalism of the statement
[
75
, Proposition 38]
. Fix two neighbouring quads
z,z^{\prime}
(at a distance
O(\delta)
from each other and
z^{\prime\prime}\in\partial B(z,R)
for
R\geq\text{cst}\cdot\delta
. Denote
\textrm{hm}^{(s),z^{\prime\prime}}_{B(z,R)}(z)
the harmonic measure of the point
z^{\prime\prime}
seen from
z
for the walk on
\mathcal{S}^{(s)}-i\mathcal{Q}^{(s)}
, that is the unique harmonic function inside
B(z,R)
whose boundary value along
\partial B(z,R)
is
0
except at
z^{\prime\prime}
where it is one. For the critical square lattice corresponding to
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
, we it is proven in
[
26
, Proposition 2.7]
that
|\textrm{hm}^{(s),z^{\prime\prime}}_{B(z,R)}(z)-\textrm{hm}^{(s),z^{\prime\prime}}_{B(z,R)}(z^{\prime})|=O(\delta^{2}R^{-2})
. The transition weights of
\textrm{hm}^{(s),z^{\prime\prime}}_{B(z,R)}
on the square lattice
\mathcal{S}^{(0)}
vary continuously with time, with an explicit dependence on the geometry of the embedding recalled in
[
15
, Equation (2.20)]
. Computing stochastic derivatives in time of
\textrm{hm}^{(s),z^{\prime\prime}}_{B(z,R)}(z)-\textrm{hm}^{(s),z^{\prime\prime}}_{B(z,R)}(z^{\prime})
, applying the Gronwall lemma (with high probability with respect to the random environment) similarly to (
4.7
), it not hard to see that
\sup_{z\sim z^{\prime}\in B(z,R/2)}|\textrm{hm}^{(s),z^{\prime\prime}}_{B(z,R)}(z)-\textrm{hm}^{(s),z^{\prime\prime}}_{B(z,R)}(z^{\prime})|
doesn’t get multiplied by more than an absolute multiplicative constant (compared to its original value at time
s=0
) for
0\leq s\leq n^{-\frac{2}{3}+\alpha}
, uniformly in
z\sim z^{\prime}
in
B(z,R/2)
. Note that to run the proof of Section
6
, one only needs a weaker statement and prove that harmonic functions on S-graphs are
(\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon)
-Holder.
∎

A.1.
Derivatives of correlations and Kadanoff-Ceva fermions

We regroup in this section the computations appearing when differentiating Kadanoff-Ceva fermions with respect to the associated coupling constants. Recall that, given two corners
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\in\Upsilon^{\times}
, the two point fermions can be written as
\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle_{S}=\mathbb{E}[\prod\limits_{e\in\gamma}(x_{e})^{\varepsilon_{e}}\sigma_{u^{\circ}(\mathfrak{p})}\sigma_{u^{\circ}(\mathfrak{q})}]
where
\gamma
is a disorder path linking
v^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{p})
to
v^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{q})
. This formula allows to compute the derivatives of
\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle_{S}
with respect to the Ising weight
x_{e_{r}}=\tan\frac{\theta_{e_{r}}}{2}
. The obtained formula in fact depends on whether both corners
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}
belong to the quad attached to the edge
x_{e_{r}}
or not. It is fairly known statement (see
[
66
]
) that given a finite set
A
and setting
\sigma_{A}:=\prod_{u\in A}\sigma_{u}
, one has

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{e_{r}}}\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{A}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2x_{e_{r}}}\big(\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{A}]\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\mathbb{E}[\sigma_{A}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]\big).

(A.1)

Similarly to the construction presented in Section
2.5
, one can use the identity
x_{e_{k}}^{\varepsilon_{e_{k}}}=\frac{1}{2}\big((x_{e_{k}}+x_{e_{k}}^{-1})\varepsilon_{e_{k}}+(x_{e_{k}}-x_{e_{k}}^{-1})\big)
to expand the two point fermion as in (
2.22
) to rewrite it as a linear combination of spins correlation. When differentiating with respect to the coupling constant
x_{e_{r}}
, some dichotomy appears, depending on the possibility to connect
\mathfrak{p}
to
\mathfrak{q}
using a disorder path that avoids the edge
e_{r}
. More precisely, one gets:

•

If the two corners
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}
don’t belong
simultaneously
to the quad
z_{r}
attached to the edge
e_{r}
, one can expand
\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle_{S}
using a disorder path linking
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}
and avoiding the edge
e_{r}
. Therefore, one can differentiate all the obtained Ising correlations with respect to
x_{e_{r}}
using (
A.1
) and then sum them back together, which implies directly that

\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{e_{r}}}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle=\frac{1}{2x_{e_{r}}}\big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}\rangle\big),

(A.2)

as all the coefficients in the correlation expansion of the products of the form
x_{e_{k}}^{\varepsilon_{e_{k}}}=\frac{1}{2}\big((x_{e_{k}}+x_{e_{k}}^{-1})\varepsilon_{e_{k}}+(x_{e_{k}}-x_{e_{k}}^{-1})\big)
present in the disorder line from
v^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{p})
to
v^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{q})
do
not
depend on the coupling constant
x_{e_{r}}
(and therefore one only needs to differentiate the Ising correlations). This allows to rewrite

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{e_{r}}}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(x_{e_{r}}+x_{e_{r}}^{-1})\cdot\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}\rangle\Big)

(A.3)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{r}})}\big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}\rangle\big)

(A.4)

•

There is a slight twist in the previous reasoning in the case where both corners
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}
belong to the edge
e_{r}
, as one can take the simplest disorder being either empty or only containing the edge
e_{r}
. When differentiating, the coefficients in
(x_{e_{r}})^{\pm 1}
now also depend on
e_{r}
. We prefer to use here another approach, with a disorder path from
\mathfrak{p}
to
\mathfrak{q}
that avoids the edge
e_{r}
. The tradeoff of this approach is that the parity of the number of crossings (between primal and dual paths) now changes when using this new path avoids
e_{r}
, meaning that a path with the same parity of crossing will connect
\mathfrak{p}
to
\mathfrak{q}^{\star}
, the other corner of
\Upsilon^{\times}
having the same planar projection as
\mathfrak{q}
. Using the spinor identity of Kadanoff-Ceva fermions leads to

\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{e_{r}}}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle=-\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{e_{r}}}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}^{\star}}\rangle=-\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{r}})}\big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}\rangle\big).

(A.5)

As a special case of correlation functions, one can enhance (
A.1
) and deduce that for any edge
e_{k}
one has

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial_{\theta_{r}}}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{r}})}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]\mathbb{E}_{S(s)}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]\Bigg)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial_{\theta_{r}}^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{\cos(\theta_{e_{r}})}{2\sin^{2}(\theta_{e_{r}})}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]\Bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{r}})}\Bigg[\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{r}})}\cdot\Bigg(\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]\Bigg)\cdot\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{r}})}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]\cdot\Bigg(\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]^{2}-1\Bigg)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{r}})}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]\Bigg)\cdot\Bigg[-\frac{\cos(\theta_{e_{r}})}{2\sin^{2}(\theta_{e_{r}})}+\frac{1}{4\sin^{2}(\theta_{e_{r}})}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{r}})}\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]\Bigg].

Let us now compute second order derivatives of Kadanoff-Ceva correlator, only focusing on differentiating twice with respect to the angle
\theta_{e_{r}}
. Assume first that
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{q}
don’t belong to the same quad
z_{r}
. In that case, one has. Let us differentiate (
A.3
) with respect to
\theta_{e_{r}}
. One has

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta^{2}_{e_{r}}}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{-\cos(\theta_{e_{r}})}{2\sin^{2}(\theta_{e_{r}})}\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{r}})}\Bigg[\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{r}})}\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}\rangle\Big)\cdot\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]

\displaystyle\quad+\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle\cdot\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{r}})}\Big(\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]^{2}-1\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{r}})}\cdot\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{r}})}\Bigg[\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}\rangle\Bigg]\cdot\Bigg[\frac{-\cos(\theta_{r})}{\sin(\theta_{r})}+\frac{\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]}{\sin(\theta_{r})}\Bigg],

using that
\varepsilon_{e_{r}}^{2}=1
. Therefore one has

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta^{2}_{e_{r}}}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle=\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{r}})}\Bigg[\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}\rangle\Bigg]\cdot\Bigg[\frac{-\cos(\theta_{r})}{\sin(\theta_{r})}+\frac{\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]}{\sin(\theta_{r})}\Bigg].

When
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\in z_{r}
one can use again the trick of changing the disorder line avoiding the edge
e_{r}
up to a sign flip which ensures that

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta^{2}_{e_{r}}}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle=-\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{r}})}\Bigg[\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}\rangle\Bigg]\cdot\Bigg[\frac{-\cos(\theta_{r})}{\sin(\theta_{r})}+\frac{\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]}{\sin(\theta_{r})}\Bigg].

We now use those first and second order derivatives to prove Lemma
6.1
, that states that as long as the deformation of the
s
-embedding doesn’t degenerate too much from the critical square lattice, all
s
-holomorphic function remain Lipschitz.

A.2.
Second order derivatives of embedding fermions

The goal of the second part of the Appendix is to present some formal computations of second order derivatives (differentiating twice with respect to the same coupling constant) when running the embedding ODE (
3.8
) with constant masses. In particular, we remain at formal level and only focus on how local relations factor out. Fix a set of masses
(m_{k})_{k\in\Lambda_{n}}
and smooth function
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Y}(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{|\Lambda_{n}|}):E(\Lambda_{n})\to(\mathbb{C})^{\Upsilon^{\times}\cap\Lambda_{n}}
such that

\frac{d\mathcal{Y}(\mathfrak{p})}{d\theta_{k}}(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{|\Lambda_{n}|})=\frac{m_{k}}{2}\Big(\mathcal{Y}(c^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S}-\mathcal{Y}(a^{+}_{k})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c^{+}_{k}}\rangle_{S}\Big),

(A.6)

where
S=S(\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{|\Lambda_{n}|})
is the full-plane Ising model with critical coupling constant
\frac{\pi}{4}
outside of
\Lambda_{n}
and coupling constants given by
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{|\Lambda_{n}|}
inside
\Lambda_{n}
. To lighten notations, we skip the
S
subscript for the Kadanoff-Ceva correlators written below, but they are all implicitly evaluated for the Ising models with weights induced by
S
.
We are going to prove that for any
\mathfrak{p}\in\Lambda_{n}
one has

\frac{d^{2}\mathcal{Y}(\mathfrak{p})}{d^{2}\theta_{k}}=\frac{m_{k}^{2}}{2}\Bigg(\frac{\cos(\theta_{e_{k}})}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}-\frac{\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\Bigg)\Bigg[\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle-\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\Bigg].

(A.7)

Let us compute
\frac{d^{2}\mathcal{Y}(\mathfrak{p})}{d^{2}\theta_{k}}
, by differentiating (
A.6
) with respect to
\theta_{k}
. One has

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\mathcal{Y}(\mathfrak{p})}{d^{2}\theta_{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{k}}{2}\Bigg[\frac{d\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})}{d\theta_{k}}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle+\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})\frac{d}{d\theta_{k}}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle

(A.8)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{d\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})}{d\theta_{k}}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle-\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})\frac{d}{d\theta_{k}}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\Bigg]

Recall that derivative formulae for Kadanoff-Ceva fermions depend a priori on whether the two associated corners belong or not the same quad. One should therefore treat separately two cases, depending on whether
\mathfrak{p}\not\in z_{k}
or
\mathfrak{p}\in z_{k}
.

Case A:
\mathfrak{p}\not\in z_{k}
. In that case one can plug (
A.3
) into (
A.8
) and write

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\mathcal{Y}(\mathfrak{p})}{d^{2}\theta_{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{k}}{2}\Bigg[\frac{m_{k}}{2}\Big(\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})\langle\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle-\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})\Big)\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{m_{k}}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\rangle\Big)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{m_{k}}{2}\Bigg[+\frac{m_{k}}{2}\Big(\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})-\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})\langle\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\Big)\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{m_{k}}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\rangle\Big)\Bigg].

One can then regroup together the
\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})
and
\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})
terms and rewrite
\frac{d^{2}\mathcal{Y}(\mathfrak{p})}{d^{2}\theta_{k}}
as

\displaystyle\frac{m_{k}^{2}}{4}\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})\Bigg[\langle\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle+\frac{1}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\rangle\Big)-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\Bigg]

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{k}^{2}}{4}\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})\Bigg[-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle+\langle\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle-\frac{1}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\rangle\Big)\Bigg].

This is where some additional simplifications appear. First note that
\varepsilon_{e_{k}}=\chi_{d_{k}^{+}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}
. Moreover, when can look at the 3 terms identity (
2.6
) for the fermion
\mathfrak{q}\mapsto\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{\mathfrak{q}}\rangle
around the quad
z_{k}
when
\mathfrak{p}\not\in z_{k}
is fixed. More specifically an analog of
(\widetilde{\mathrm{D}}_{k}^{(\mathfrak{p})})
when moving the second argument around
z_{k}
ensures that

-\frac{1}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\rangle-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle=\frac{\cos(\theta_{e_{k}})}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle.

(A.9)

Similarly writing
\varepsilon_{e_{k}}=\chi_{b_{k}^{+}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}
together with an analog of relation
(\widetilde{\mathrm{B}}_{k}^{(\mathfrak{p})})
when moving the second fermion around
z_{k}
ensures that

\frac{1}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\rangle-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle=-\frac{\cos(\theta_{e_{k}})}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle.

(A.10)

which allows to rewrite
\frac{d^{2}\mathcal{Y}(\mathfrak{p})}{d^{2}\theta_{k}}
as

\displaystyle\frac{m_{k}^{2}}{4}\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle\Big[\langle\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle-\frac{\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}+\frac{\cos(\theta_{e_{k}})}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{k}^{2}}{4}\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\Big[\langle\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle+\frac{\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}-\frac{\cos(\theta_{e_{k}})}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\Big]

One can conclude using the three term identity around the quad
z_{k}
(which holds here on
\Upsilon^{\times}_{(a_{k})}
) and deduce that

\langle\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle=-\langle\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle=-\frac{\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}+\frac{\cos(\theta_{e_{k}})}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}

(A.11)

This allows to conclude that, when
\mathfrak{p}\not\in z_{k}
(
A.7
) indeed holds.

Case B:
\mathfrak{p}\in z_{k}
. In that case one can plug (
A.5
) into (
A.8
). Changing the sign of the correlator derivative allows to rewrite

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\mathcal{Y}(\mathfrak{p})}{d^{2}\theta_{k}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{k}}{2}\Bigg[\frac{m_{k}}{2}\Big(\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})\langle\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle-\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})\Big)\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{m_{k}}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\rangle\Big)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{m_{k}}{2}\Bigg[+\frac{m_{k}}{2}\Big(\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})-\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})\langle\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\Big)\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{m_{k}}{2\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\rangle\Big)\Bigg].

One regroups once again the
\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})
and
\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})
terms together and rewrite
\frac{d^{2}\mathcal{Y}(\mathfrak{p})}{d^{2}\theta_{k}}
as

\displaystyle\frac{m_{k}^{2}}{4}\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})\Bigg[\langle\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle-\frac{1}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\rangle\Big)-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\Bigg]

\displaystyle+\frac{m_{k}^{2}}{4}\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})\Bigg[-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle+\langle\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle+\frac{1}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\rangle\Big)\Bigg].

Similarly to the case where
\mathfrak{p}\not\in z_{k}
, one can use the local relations for Kadanoff-Ceva correlators near their singularities. Let us work out the case
\mathfrak{p}=a_{k}^{+}
in details, the others can be treated exactly similarly. In that case one can rewrite
\frac{d^{2}\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})}{d^{2}\theta_{k}}
as

\frac{m_{k}^{2}}{4}\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})\Bigg[\langle\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle-\langle\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\Bigg]+\\
\frac{m_{k}^{2}}{4}\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})\Bigg[-1+\langle\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle^{2}+\frac{1}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\Big(\langle\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]-\langle\chi_{d_{k}^{+}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\Big)\Bigg],

where we used once again the identities
\langle\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle=1
,
\varepsilon_{e_{k}}=\chi_{d_{k}^{+}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}
and the anti-symmetry of the fermions when exchanging their arguments. Using this time the local relations around
z_{k}
given by
(\textrm{D}_{k}^{\prime\prime})
and the local energy density identity (
A.11
) ensures that

\frac{d^{2}\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})}{d^{2}\theta_{k}}=\frac{m_{k}^{2}}{2}\Bigg(\frac{\cos(\theta_{e_{k}})}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}-\frac{\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]}{\sin(\theta_{e_{k}})}\Bigg)\Bigg[\mathcal{Y}(c_{k}^{+})\langle\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle-\mathcal{Y}(a_{k}^{+})\langle\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle\Bigg].

(A.12)

The main application of these formulae is some explicit formulae for the second order derivative of the fermion encoding the embedding when constructed as a solution to (
3.8
). More precisely, assume that
\mathcal{Y}
is a solution to (
3.8
) in
\Lambda_{n}
, with all the masses in
\Lambda_{n}
given by the collection
(m_{k})_{k\in\Lambda_{n}}
. Then for
\mathfrak{p}\in\Lambda_{n}
, one can easily see that

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(\mathfrak{p})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{e_{k}\in\Lambda_{n}}\frac{m_{k}^{2}}{2}\Bigg(\frac{\cos(\theta^{(t)}_{e_{k}})}{\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{e_{k}})}-\frac{\mathbb{E}_{S(t)}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]}{\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{e_{k}})}\Bigg)\Bigg[\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c_{k}^{+})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}-\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a_{k}^{+})\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}\Bigg]

(A.13)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum\limits_{e_{k}\neq e_{r}\in\Lambda_{n}}m_{k}\cdot m_{r}\cdot g^{(\mathfrak{p})}_{k,r}(t),

(A.14)

where
g^{(\mathfrak{p})}_{k,r}(t)
is given by (using the Pfaffian rules and voluntarily not written in a more-simplified manner as the involved contributions will disappear when one plugs it in the proof of Lemma
5.1
due to independence of Brownian motions at different edges
e_{k}\neq e_{r}
)

\displaystyle g^{(\mathfrak{p})}_{k,r}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}\Big(\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c_{r}^{+})\langle\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\chi_{a_{r}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}-\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a_{r}^{+})\langle\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\chi_{c_{r}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c_{k}^{+})\cdot\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{e_{r}})}\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}\mathbb{E}_{S(t)}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}\rangle_{S(t)}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{4}\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}\Big(\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c_{r}^{+})\langle\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\chi_{a_{r}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}-\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a_{r}^{+})\langle\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\chi_{c_{r}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a_{k}^{+})\cdot\frac{1}{2\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{e_{r}})}\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}\mathbb{E}_{S(t)}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}\rangle_{S(t)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\quad\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c_{r}^{+})\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{r}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{r}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}\mathbb{E}_{S(t)}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a_{r}^{+})\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{r}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{k}}\rangle_{S(t)}-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{r}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}\mathbb{E}_{S(t)}[\varepsilon_{e_{k}}]\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(c_{k}^{+})}{2\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{e_{r}})}\Big(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}\mathbb{E}_{S(t)}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{a_{k}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}\rangle_{S(t)}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\mathcal{Y}^{(t)}(a_{k}^{+})}{2\sin(\theta^{(t)}_{e_{r}})}\Bigg(\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\rangle_{S(t)}\mathbb{E}_{S(t)}[\varepsilon_{e_{r}}]-\langle\chi_{\mathfrak{p}}\chi_{c_{k}^{+}}\varepsilon_{e_{r}}\rangle_{S(t)}\Big).

(A.15)
